The Honorable James F. Byrnes,

894.92/10
Dear Mr. Byrnes:-
This is in response to your request sent to me through the
Honorable D. Emmert Brumbaugh of the House of Representatives
in which you asked for data on G. Asahara and Kotaro Honda.
G. Asahara studied in mission schools in Japan where he had a
good chance to get the typical "western" viewpoint. I first
ran across him in Schenectady, New York. At that time I was
a research physicist at the General Electric Research Labora-
tory and Asahara and Shoji Nishikawa were in the graduate
school of Cornell University. About 1918 these two men were
working in the chemistry department at Cornell on the dif-
fraction of x-rays by crystals and came to Schenectady to see
my work along similar lines. It seems to be the custom of
/10-2545
foreign scientists visiting in this country to try to get as
much information as possible and to give as little as possible
in return. Contrary to the usual custom they started out the
conference by telling of their own work at Cornell. I com-
plimented them upon their observance of the American"rule of
50-50". Asaharais reply was that he had studied with
Americans and English for so long that he had come to apprec-
iate their point of view. At the end of the conference I in-
vited both of these men to my home for dinner, telling them
that they were the first two Japanese I had ever seen who ob-
served the American rule of 50-50. In 1938 Mrs. Davey and I
had occasion to visit Japan and Drs. Asahara and Nishikawa
were most kind to us. They made it a point to help me to see
CS/VJ
as much as possible of the physics work of the University of
Tokio and of the Institute of Physical and Chemical Research of
Tokio. Of course, there was probably an element of "saving
face" in this because they felt under the necessity of re-
turning our hospitality, but, as nearly as I can judge,thei
expression of friendship had a very sincere ring. Duringur
visit Asahara mentioned the matter of strained-international
relationships to me at a time when Mrs. Davey was not witan
894.42
to the Japanese armed aggression in China. I said furthe
that if, instead, the Japanesehad confined themselves to economic
penetration the Americans would have admired them for being

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
Hon. James F. Byrnes
-2-
0ct.25,1945.
good business men. Dr. Asahara replied that he had favored only
economic penetration but that he had been outvoted. Later on
when I was out of earshot he approached Mrs. Davey on the matter
of "strained international relation". She gave him substantially
the same answer I had given him and in turn received from him
substantially the same reply.
At the time of our visit Asahara was in charge of the Japanese
Economic Exploitation of Manchucuo. He would fly from Tokio
to Manchucuo, stay there three days and fly back to Tokio for
the remainder of the week. He seemed to be interested not
only in mining and metallurgical work but also in increasing the
yield of crops in Manchucuo. He had just completed the purchase
from the Graham-Paige Co. of a complete set of manufacturing
drawings for their previous years model of passenger automobile.
It was my understanding that Asahara could not interview the
Mikado directly but that he could and did interview those men
in Tokio who were permitted to talk directly to the Mikado.
In-so-far as I am able to judge Asahara might prove to be of
definite assistance to General MacArthur's staff.
I first met Dr. Kotaro Honda when he visited the Research
Laboratory of the General Electric Co. at Schenectady, N. Y.
In accordance with the usual custom of foreigners visiting
American laboratories he tried to get as much information as
possible and give as little in return as possible. It was
only after a reference to the American rule of 50-50 that he
gave us any considerable amount of information. I next ran
across Honda at the Boston (1926) meeting of the American
Society for Steel Treating, (now called American Society for
Metals). At this meeting with tears streaming down his cheeks,
he begged for peaceful relations between Japan and the United
States. In my opinion he was "putting on an act". Honda is
one of the best, if not the best, research metallurgist in the
world. He is President of Tohoku Imperial University at
Sendai in Northern Japan. He is at the same time Director of
the Research Institute for Iron, Steel and Other Metals in
Sendai and is in charge of one of the research laboratories in
the Institute of Physical and Chemical Research in Tokio. His
research laboratory at Sendai is one of the best, (probably
the best), university metallurgical research laboratories in
the world. I visited his Research Institute for Iron, Steel
and Other Metals during my visit to Japan in 1938. I was
given a good look at the facilities of his laboratory but was
shotn as little of the details as possible. We were invited
to lunch at the Institute together with some of the high ranking
members of the research staff. The atmosphere at this lunch

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.99)
Hon. James F. Byrnes
-3-
Oct. 25, 1945.
was very decidedly hostile. After lunch instead of continuing
the visit to the laboratory, we were put in an automobile and
shown some of the beautiful scenery on the coast north of
Sendai.
In my opinion Dr. Honda and his staff should be watched very
carefully. .They will appear to cooperate if they feel that
they are compelled to but from their attitude at the time of
my visit I believe that the cooperation will never be any deeper
than they feel is absolutely necessary.
In accordance with your request I am sending herewith my copy
of the Guide to the Institute of Physical and Chemical Research
dated 1929. This Guide gives the names of the members of the
staffs of the various aivisions of the Institute and the topics
on which they were doing research as of that date. In-so-far as
I can remember the various researchs which I was shown at the
time of my visit in 1938, these topics had not changed much
since 1929. I have taken the liberty of marking with a dash
those research topics which obviously have, or might have, a
military bearing and I have marked with an arrow those topics which
either are or could be of use in the study of atomic energy.
I am also enclosing a copy of the Conspectus of the Research
Institute for Iron, Steel and Other Metals, which contains a
detailed list of the staff of that Institute. An appendix
to the Conspectus is a list of the authors of various publi-
cations made by the Institute together with the topics on
which these authors have worked. It is my opinion that this
bibliography represents only that fraction of the work of the
laboratory which its staff feels can be made public.
Very truly yours,
WheelerP. Dovry
Wheeler P. Davey
nesearch Professor of Physics
and Chemistry

I have the honor to transmit, as of interest to the Department,
Cn
a memorandum prepared by a member of the staff of this Office on the
subject: "Educational Affairs in Japan, October 1 to November 8, 1945".
The memorandum describes the principal directives relating to
education issued to the Japanese Government by the Supreme Commander
or the Allied Powers, mentions the cooperative attitude of the Japanese
authorities, and includes a selection of comment from the Japanese
CS/LE
press indicative of a general interest in educational reforms.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 49 of November 10, 1945, from the Acting
United States Political Adviser to the Supreme Commander for the
AAlied Powers, Tokyo, Japan, on the subject "Transmission of
Memorandum on Educational Affairs in Japan, October 1 to Movember
8. 1945".
MEMORANDOM
EDUCATIONAL AFFAIRS IN JAPAN
October 1 to November 8, 1945.
1. Three directives concerning education have been issued to the
Japanese Government by the Supreme Headquarters for the Allied Powers.
"Administration of the Educational System of Japan"
(October 22, 1945).
"Violation of Religious Freedom" (October 24, 1945).
"Investigation, Screening, and Certification of Teachers
and Educational Officers" (October 30, 1945).
Copies of these directives are attached to this memorandum.
In addition, the directive of October 4, 1945, on the subject
"Removal of Restrictions on Political, Civil, and Religious Liberties".
has general reference to educational policies and objectives. This
directive was transmitted to the Department under cover of Despatch
No. 2, October 8, 1945.
2. The three educational directives listed above may be
characterized as follows:
(a). The first states the general concepts and policies. Courses
of inatruction are to be examined and revised to eliminate the dissemina-
tion of militaristic and ultra-nationalistic ideology and also military
education and drill. The objective of the educational program is to be
the inculcation of concepts and the establishment of practices in harmony
with representative government, international peace, the dignity of the
individual, and such fundamental human rights as freedom of assembly.
speech, and religion.
It was directed that the personnel of all edncational institu-
tions be screened.
(b). The third, as listed above, gives more detailed instructions
regarding

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
一 2 
regarding the elimination from the educational system of Japan of
"those militaristic and ultra-nationalistic influences which in the
past have contributed to the defeat, war guilt, suffering, privation
and present deplorable state of the Japanese people.:......". The
Japanese Ministry of Education is requirod to set up the machinery to
attain these ends and to submit its plans for complying with this
memorandum.
(c). The Nippon Times, November 3, 1945, printed a clarifying
statement from Colonel Ken R. Dyke (Chief of the Civil Information and
Education Section of Supreme Headquarters). The article states that
instructors now teaching and not known to be militaristic or ultra-
nationalistic will remain at their posts pending screening. No whole
sale dismissal of instructors was intended. Teachers who had served in
the armed forces, but who are not career military or naval personnel
will not necessarily be dismissed or barred from employment.
The second above, "Violation of Religious Freedom", refers
to "certain acts on the part of officials of educational institutions,
founded and supported by Christians of foreign nations, which represent
inexcusable and unjustifiable subversion of such institutions to
militaristic and ultra-nationalistic ends".
It refers to the situation at Rikkyo Gakuin (St. Paul's
University and Middle Schools). It directs the Japanese Government to
dismiss immediately the president and other officials and professors
named. The memorandum directs that "Rikkyo Gakuin be reorganized and
It is required that a survey be conducted of "all educational
institutions in Japan which were founded and maintained by missions on
public contributions of Christians of foreign nations". A report was
required by 15 November 1945. A list of institutions to be surveyed is
furnished.

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
furnished. Eighty two institutions are listed. The list is stated
to be suggestive but not inclusive.
(a). The general directive of October 4, "Removal of Restrio-
tions on Political, Civil, and Religious Liberties", is a five page
document which was issued to abolish restrictive legislation, disband
harmful organizations and to make possible the development of freedom
of speech, of press, and the development of representative institutions.
Its provisions give the general background for the revision of Japanese
education.
3. The cooperation of the Japanese Ministry of Education and of
school authorities is on the surface complete. They seem anxious to
comply with the requests and suggestions of the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers.
4. Clippings from newspapers show considerable general interest.
Nippon Timee. November 5: "Clean up of schools Set by
Government". This is a sympathetic discussion of the
directive of October 30.
Asahi Shimbun,November 2: "There is urgent need for
Educational Reform".
Nippon Times, November 1: Mitsuoke KAkIHU, writing on
suffrage for women, urges coeducation.
Lomiuri-Hochi,October 24: States that there is a ferment
of democratic ideas and that demands for change are
coming from inside various institutions, including schools
and newspapers.
Other news stories bear out this statement. Examples are
the following:
Asahi-Shimbum,October 19: A group of students at Waseda
University were reported in conflict with President
AGANO over the preferences granted to Army and Navy cadets.
The students held a mass meeting and presented a resolution.
Nippon Times. November 1: Quotes Salaro ISHIWATA of the
Peers School that it has been decided to open the schools
were cooperating and thatnew text books were being approved.
Lippon

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.99)
Hippan Times. November 3, 1945: The Tokyo University of
Industry (formerly Tokyo University of Commeroe) is try
ing democratic procedures. The student body requested
the return of a member of the faculty. The students were
forming a Student Consumers Union to take over food and
lodging problems. The students voted for the strengthen-
ing of the seminar system, and for closer relations with
the faculty. There were sim reports of students strikes
in various schools for shorter study hours and longer
weekends to go home because of the insufficiency of food.
Student strikers also were interested in the election of
heads of classes, the dismissal of certain school officials
and teachers, the return of dismissed teachers and for the
establishment of courses on civics and democracy.
Nippon Sangvo Keizal, November 4, 1945: The Economics
course in Tokyo Imperial University will be reorganised;
dismissed professors are being recalled and other professors
have resigned.
Yomiuri-Hochi, October 5, 1945: Urges that all incompetent
professors be dismissed not only for ideological reasons
but also for incompetence. The opinion was expressed that
reform of the imiversities cannot come from the administra-
tion, the faculty nor from the students. It was urged that
the public should enter into the discussion of educational
reforms and that public members should serve on the committees
considering reforms.
Three articles by Eiichi NICHIZAwA have appeared in the Hippon
Times. November 6, 7 and 8, under the title: "Education in the Past
Marked by Government Interference". They seem significant enough
to be attached.
The Qkuyama Translation Service. November 3, 1945: quotes
Premier Shidehara's speech in the Conference of Prefectural Governors,
November 2, 1945, as follows: "To respect freedom and promote the
rights of the people is the essential of our people's politics and the
realization will form the basis of the rehabilitation of Japan".
"What would form the foundation of such political system is re-
novation of education; anti-foretgn ideas or an idea of narrow-minded
self-complacency should be got rid of; and education aiming at the
completion of the self and service to the people and society should
be

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
5
be established".
Karl C. Leebrick.
Enclosures: (Transmitted in single copy only).

2. SCAP directive, October 24, 1945- "Violation of Religious
Freedom".
3. SCAP directive, October 30, 1945- "Investigation, Screening.
and Certification of Teachers and Educational Officers".
4. Series of three articles from Nippon Times, November 6-8,
1945, "Education in the Past Marked by Government Interference",
by Eiichi NICHIZAWA.

: Central Liaison Office, Tokyo.
SUBJECT
: Administration of the Educational System of Japan.
1. In crder that the newly formed Cabinet of the Imperial Japanese
Government shall be fully informed of the ob ectives and policies of the
occupation with regard to Educaticn, it is herehy directed that:
a. The content of all inst uction  ill be critically examined,
ievised, and controlled in acccidance with the folloving policies:
(1) Dissemination cf militaristic and ultra-nationalistic
ideclogy will be prchibited and all military education
and drill will be discontinued.
842
(2)
Inculcation of concepts and establishment of practices
in harmony vith representative government, international
peace, the dignity of the individual., and such fundamental
human rights as the freedom of assembly, speech, and
religion, will be encouraged.
b. The pe: sonnel of all educational institutions will be inves-
tigated, approved or removed, reinstated, appointed, reorientated, and
supervised in acccrdance with the following policies:
rapidly as possible und all career military personnel,
persons who have been active exp nents of militarism
and ultra-nationalism, and those actively antagonistic
to the policies of the occupation will be reroved.
(2)
Teachers and educational officials who have been dis-
missed, suspended, or forced to resin for liberal or
anti-militaristic opinions or activities, vill be declared
immediately eligible for and if properly qualified will
be g'ven preference in reappointment.
(3)
Discrimination against any student, teacher, or
educational official on grounds of race, nationality,
842
-1-

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
creed, political opinion, or social poeition, will be
prohibited, and immediate stens will be taken to correct
inequities which ha'e resulted from such discrimination.
(4)
Students, teachers, end educational officials will be
encouraged to evaluate critically and intelligently
the content of instruction and will be permitted to
engage in free and unrestricted discussion of issues
involving political, civil, and religious liberties.
(5)
Students, teachers, educational officials, and public
will be informed of the objectives and policies of the
occupation, cf the theory and practices of represen-
tative government, and of the part played by militaristic
leaders, their active collaborators, and those who by
passive acquiescence comritted the nation to war with the
inevitable result of defeat, distress, and the present
deplcrable state of the Japanese people.
c. The instrumentalities of educ: tional processes will be
critically examined, revised, and controlled in accordance vith the follow-
ing policies:
(1)
Existing curricula, textbooks, teaching manuals, and
instructional meterials, the use of vhich is temporarily
permitted on an emergency basis, will be examined as
rapidly as possible and those portions designed to pro-
mote a milita:istic or ultra-naticnalistic ideology will
be eliminated.
(2)
New curricula, teytbcoks, teaching manuals, and in-
structional materials..designed to produce an educated,
peaceful, and rcspcnsible citizenry vil? be prepa:ed and
will be substituted for cxisting materials as rapidly as
possible.
(3)
A normally operating educational system will be 1e-
established as rapidly as possible, but where limited
facilities exist prcference will be piven to elementary
educ tion and teacher training.
2. The Japanese Ministry cf Education will establish and maintain
adequate liaison with the a propriate staff section of the Office of the
Supieme Commander for the Allied Powers, and upon request will submit
reports describing in detail all action taken to comply with the provisions
of this directive.
3. All officials and subcrdinates of the Jepanese Government affected
by the torms of this directive, and all teachers and schocl officials,
bcth public and private, will be held personally accountable for com-
pliance with the spirit as well as the letter of the policies enunciated
in this diiective.
FOR THE SUPI.EME COMMANDER:

24 October 1945
MEMORANDUM FOR:
IMPERIAL JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
THROUGH
Central Liaison Office, Tokyo.
SUBJECT
:
Violation of Religious Freedom.
1. The attention of this headquarters has been directed
to certain acts on the part of officials of educational in-
stitutions, founded and supported by Christians of foreign
nations, which represent inexcusable and unjustifiable subver-
sion of such institutions to militaristic and ultra-nationalistic
ends.
2. The case of Rikkyo Gakuin (St. Paul's University and
Middle School) is cited as a specific example of such flagrant
violation of religious freedom and unwarranted vandalism.
a. Originally founded in 1874, Rikkyo Gakuin was given
a charter on 7 July 1931 by the Japanese Government. Articles
840.4
of the Act of Incorporation state "the object of the Zaidan is
to administer education in Japan in accordance with the prin-
ciples of Christianity . . ."
b. In 1943, certain officials of the university,
ignoring their oblig tions to maintain a Christian institution
of learning, did, without provocation and with or without the
approval of the Ministry of Education, abolish Christian
services and teachings; caused the removal from office of
Christian members of the faculty and the Board of Directors, and
caused to be committod acts of vandalism in All Saints' Memorial
Chapel and against other property of the university. Christi-
anity was abolished by order in Rikkyo and the university Chapel
was closed in January 1943.
3. No action has been taken by university officials or by
the Ministry of Education since the termination of the war to
rectify this violation of freedom of religion and of moral trust.
4. The Imperial Japanese Government is directed immediately
to:
a. Dismiss from their present positions, the following
officials of Rikkyo Gakuin:
(1)
Kinzo Sambe, President (Socho)
(2)
Hidesaburo Hoashi, Dean (Gakkan) of the
University and Principal of the Middle
School

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.98)
(3)
Soichi Tsuji, Dean of the Yoka bu
(4)
Hisckazu Kaneki, Student Inspector
(5)
H. J. Miyazaki, 'Student Inspector
(6)
Atsuo Ozawa, Student Inspector
(7)
Ryo Shibata, Student Inspector
(8)
Ko Ogata, teacher, former Student Inspector
(9)
Captain Wada, Acting Dean, Science Department
(10) Yasuo Muto, Librarian
(11) Miotaro Abe, Teacher, former Student Inspector
b. Direct that none of tho individuals designated in
paragraph 4a be rc-employed or placed in any position in any

(a). The first states the general concepts and policies. Gourses

with representative government, international peace, the digmity of the
individual, and such fundamental human rights as freedom of assembly.
speech, and religion.

Enclosure to despatch no. 84 of December 5, 1945, from
the Acting United States Political Adviser, Tokyo, Japan,
on the subject "Observations of Educational Institutions
in Various Cities of Japan."
MEMORANDUM
November 27, 1945.
SUBJECT: Observations of Educational Institutions in
Various Cities other than Tokyo.
This report is based on conferences and observations made
during visits which took place November 19 - 25, 1945, to Kyoto,
Nara, Osaka, Kobe and Nagoya. The institutions visited were:
Kyoto Imperial University, Doshisha University, Osaka Imperial
University, Imamiya Chu-gakko (Osaka, Middle School for boys),
Yuhigakko jo-gakko (Sun-set Hill Girls High School, Osaka), and
a number of elementary and middle school buildings in the cities
mentioned above.
University conditions end probleme:
Faculties have not been seriously depleted. Faculty
members, considered essential by university administrations were
usually kept at their posts even though called by the armed ser
vices. A material number of faculty members, considered un-
desirable by the war government appointed university administre-
tion, were forced to resign or retire. Some of these faculty
men are now being recalled. This is true at Kyoto and at other
Imperial Universities. Osaka Imperial University and Doshisha
University report that they had no forced resignations of faculty.
The salaries of the faculties are so low that it is almost
impossible for them to live under present living costs. Several
faculty members showed the results of malnutrition.
Transportation congestion makes it difficult for faculty
and students to travel to and from the universities. In some
cases this has resulted in shortened hours and in longer midday
periods.
In damaged cities, students have found it difficult to find
living quarters and to obtain even the current rati ons of foods.
Student enrollment has fallen in all universities because of
service in the Army and Navy and because of the difficulty of
attendance in damaged cities. Students in small numbers are be-
ginning to return to universities. Face-saving figures of above
eighty percent of pre-war enrollment were given. There is reason
to doubt this high figure.
Student organizations have sprung up in most universities.
Osaka Imperial University and Doshisha University report very
little student activity to date. At Kyoto Imperial University
the organized students requested; change in the administration
and in the faculty; more contact between teachers and students;
shortening of hours of instruction;and that something be done to
secure

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
secure them more food and better living quarters. The students
have also requested the re-establishment of "cultural and social
science" courses. The authorities consulted were not alarmed
about these student movements. It was felt that the increasingly
stringent restrictions placed on the students during the long war
period had over-regimented the students. The current movements
were considered a normal reaction.
Curricula changes since the end of the war are slight. These
universities have added cultural courses for students and adults,
a few of these courses are open to occupational forces. Some
courses established by the wartime government have been discontinued
as for instance atomic research courses and several relating to
areo-mechanics. Social science courses and seminar type discussion
classes have been organized.
Library and instructional books and teaching aids are much
needed. There is an expressed desire for recent books in all fields.
Library files remind one that Japan has been cut off from most of
the world for about five years.
The buildings of Kyoto Imperial University were undamaged.
Property damage at Osaka Imperial University was extensive. The
medical building was burned. Medical instruction is now carried
on in the damaged hospital. The main building lost many windows
and suffered some fire damage. This building was over full of
salvaged equipment. The building maintenance was very poor. There
is class-room space for the present enrollment. This institution
is really a technical college or university; for the physical and
natural sciences, for mechanical and electrical engineering, and
for medicine. The former president, a career navy officer, retired,
last spring because of age. A former professor of chemistry is
now president.
Schools below the university level:
Curricula changes have been made to comply with occupational
authority directives. The "morals courses" are reported discontinued
as are also the instruction in military courses and drill. There
is information, however, that all the military drill has not been
eliminated from some schools. The teachers of Osaka Prefecture
are meeting by districts to study and propose text books deletions
and additions.
Faculty changes are taking place. A few wartime appointed
principals have been replaced. A small number of teachers who
served in the armed services have been re-employed. A few temporary
women teachers, who took the wartime six months preparatory course,
have been released. No shortage of teachers seems to exist.
In the Osaka Prefecture the teachers were called together,
by districts, by representatives of the national educational
authorities and were given instructions about their post war teach-
ing attitude and responsibilities.
Salaries are so low that they do not provide for the bare
necessities.
There

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
There is a real shortage of text books, old or new, and of
all supplementary instructional material and equipment.
School attendance is difficult for students and teachers
because of traffic congestion and the long distances which must
be travelled to reach unburned sections of the cities.
Food deficiencies are real. Children show signs of malnutri-
tion. Physical exercises has of necessity been restricted. Schools
have shortened the school day to permit the students to eat at home.
In several schools instruction ceases about midday.
Clothing is also inadequate.
School maintenance, as observed, was very poor. The buildings
were dusty and dirty, with the exception of Sunset Hill Girl's High
School at Osaka. Most institutions except at Kyoto and Nara suffered
bomb or fire damage. In those cities there is some crowding in te
schools below university grade, due to influx of population from bombed
cities.
School officials reported they need more instructions and assis-
tance from their own or from occupational authorities. The impression
was given me that school administrators and teachers are hesitant,
or perhaps afraid, to act on their own initiative for fear of later
rebuke or punishment.
KCLeebrickmc

ASSISTAN
Office Memorandum
UNMEED STAERS GOVERNMENT
TO
A-B Mr. William Benton
STATE
FROM
OIC William T. Stone
SUBJECT:
Department's responsibility for re-education policy in Japan
As a result of the action taken by the sWncc, placing
responsibility on the Department for basic planning and develop
ment of the U.S. program of reorientation and re-education in
Japan, I requested a meeting with the Bureau of the Budget on
February 21.
Present at the meeting were Messrs Rosepman, Campbell,
and Lawson of the Budget, and Schuler and myself for OIC.
 pointed out that the OIc budget and staff approved by the
Budget Bureau was totally inadequate to carry out the responsi-
bility now placed upon the Department by SWNCC. The present
staff for work on Japan in Washington consists of only six
officers (two of which are junior assistants)and three clerks.
The minimum personnel required to work on formulation of long-
term policy and basic planning would be twelve to thirteen
officers and nine or ten clerical and administrative assistants.
In addition, it will be necessary to call together the best
qualified authorities available in the United States for advice
and assistance in the formulation of policy statements nd basic
planning.
In view of the urgency attached to this program by
SWNCC I asked the Budget Bureau to advise us informally on the
following points:
1) Is it permissible for us to tell the Appropriations
Committee at our hearings of the increased responsibility
placed on the Department in this field?
-2296
(The representatives of the Budget Bureau agreed
RM/R
that we could inform the Appropriations Committee
Anal.
of the SWNCC paper and pointmi out the increased
Rev.
responsibility which it places upon us.)
Cat.L
2) Would it be possible for us to request additional
funds for our occupation division in view of the fact that
the SWNCC action had been taken after our budget was sent
to'Congress?
(The representatives of the Budget Bureau
pointed out that we could not alter the estimates
sent to Congress without again securing the formal

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
approval of the Director of the Budget and the
President. This would require considerable time
and would probably be turned down on the ground
that the Secretary of State could provide the
additional amounts from his total budget.)
3) If we do not submit the revised estimate now
could we advise the Appropriations Committee that we will
probably come up for a supplemental estimate la ter in the
year?
(The Budget Bureau representatives agreed
that we could make such a statement. They suggested
that we use the case of Japan to point out the need
for fle xibility in our entire program; that we could
emphasize the importance of our responsibility for
policy in Japan, Germany and other occupied areas
and point out that similar responsibilities are
likely to be placed on the Department in related
fields; therefore, we can only do the job effectively
if our funds are not cut below the anger point.)
In view of the above, I propose to adopt the following
procedure:
1) To inform the Appropriations Committee (off the
record) of the SWNCC directive, stressing the importance of the
job imposed on the Department and specifically on OIC.
2) Indicate to the Committee the possibility of a
supplemental estimate if our full budget is not approved.
3) To appoint immediately an advisory committee, com-
posed of eight or ten of the best qualified people in the United
States to advise us on long-term re-education policies for Japan.
This advisory group will be selected immediately and the members
appointed as consultants or advisers to the Department. We have
funds to cover this expense until June 30, 1946. Frank Schuler
is preparing a panel of names and will be ready to discuss the
list next week. Several members of the panel may be drawn from
the advis ory group which is about to leave for Japan.
4) The advisory group will be put to work immediately
on the revision of the existing policy directive and will work
with Schuler.

TEACHERS COLLEGE

Mr. Frank A. Schuler, Jr.
Chief, Japanese-Korean Branch
FEB 24 1948
Division of Occupied Areas
BEPATRENT OLE ONE
Department of State
Washington, D. C.
Lear Mr. chuler:
On 7 February 1948 the New York Times carried a special dispatch
from Tokyo,dated February 6, under the heading "Chaplains to Scan Tokyo
Textbooks". The story states that because some of the textbook auth-
894.42/2-1848
ors in Janan have written lessons which cast some doubt on the authen-
ticity of certain of the Bible stories, pressure has been brought to
xR
have a Christian board representing the Occupation pass on such sec-
4.404
tions. It goes on to state that the Allied Headquarters has decided
to establish a board of chaplains to review all such material before
it is included in the official textbooks.
This seems to be a radical shift in policy for a number of rea-
sons. The first is that it has been an established policy in Head-
quarters fron the beginning of the occupation that chaplains in the
military services should have nothing to do with questions of reli-
gious policy in the Japanese community. The second is that Headquar-
ters annears now to have established an ecclesiastical board which is
going to determine what the Japanese shall be told about a religious
matter, clearly a violation of the religjous freedom which has been
promised the Japanese and which is basic to our entire policy. And
finally, this action seems to be a clear break with prior policy deal-
ing with the criteria of accentability of material presented for the
national textbooks, which to the best of my knowledge has never includ-
ed anything which limits the historical interpretation of the Japanese
on matters which have a relivious siznificance.
You might be interested in the reaction which I have noticed in
my classes here at the university. I have a number of students with
long residence in Japan, and in fact a number who have been Christian
missionaries in Japan, and it is the universal and very positive opin-
ion of these peonle that this is an extremely unwise policy. They
say, and my own experience would indicate, that this may prove to be
a very undesirable move. It smacks of conducting a Christian mission-
ary activity under the uise of Occupational censorship; and even for
people who have the reatest respect ror and nersonal interest in the
Christian missionary movement in Janan, such a policy appears to be
quite inconsistent with our moral and political position.
—2
2
hs
0-13

Mr. Frank A. Schuler, Jr.
-2-
18 February 1948

news dispatch is correct; and, secondly, a statement of the basic
United States policy under which such local action by the CI&E was
taken?
Very truly yours,
Kabut . e

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
"I am just in receipt of your letter of January l2th enolo-
sing a clipping from the Denver Register with reference errors
which appeared in the early volumes of the Japanese language text
of the "History of the West" which was issued for optional study
only in the higher grades of the Japanese schools. Fortunately
the errors were detected shortly after the first release and
corrective action was taken at once. In fact, the sentence to
which particular exception rightly was taken was ordered deleted,
but through a mistake by the Japanese found its way into the
Japanese text. For this failure strictly to follow instructions,
both the author and the publisher were severely disciplined. The
persons in this headquarters who pass upon the accuracy of context
bearing upon Christianity written under Japanese authorship for
school study are devout Christian leaders of several denomina-
tions - but you must understand that their task is rendered ex-
tremely difficult both by the language differences which rise to
harass all occupational effort and the need to find an acceptable
common ground of reconciliation on points in which there exist
differing views among the denominations. To provide added safe-
guard against the infiltration of similar objectionable material
into the Japanese texts, steps have now been taken to submit all
proposed discussion of Christianity to a Board of Chaplains, com-
prising all Christian denominations present... We have in our
favor the complete spiritual vacuum left in the wake of war and
defeat and destruction which obliterated the very foundations to
those false concepts on wnich the Japanese heretofore have de-
pended for guidance, sustenance and protection. Through daily
contact with our American men and women who are here engaged in
tne reshaping of Japan's future, there is penetrating into the
Japanese mind the noble influences which find their origin and
their inspiration in the American home. These influences are
rapidly bearing fruit, and apart from the great numbers who are
coming to embrace the Christian Faith, a whole population is
coming to understand and cherish its underlying principles and
ideals.
"With my hearty thanks for bringing this matter so promptly
to my attention, I am,
Faithfully yours,
Douglas MacArthur"

Sir:
I have she honor to transmit copies of two memoranda
pre ented to this Office by WATANABE Chugo, "Gist of Proposed
Reform of Education" and"Practical Application of the duca-
tional Reform Plan". Mr. WATANABE is a graduate of St. Paul'
University, Tokyo. For a mumber of years he has been Director
of the Japan Trade Promotion Association. He promoted and led
several trips of Japanese businessmen to the United States. He
is now interested in encouraging political, educational, eco-
nomic, and religious reforms. Mr. WATANABE came to see us
after talking with an officer in the Education Division of
Civil Information and Education Section, SCaP, and with offi-
cials in the Japanese Ministry of Education. The suggestion
CS/LF
to talk with us came from SAkOMIZU Hisatsune, a former Cabinet
Secretary.
(Sppa.c)
The author could well have combined the two papers.
The first is a brief statement of objectives, the second an
elaboration of procedure to make the plans effective. The
proposals include: a revision of the entire school system as
to organization, administration, and curriculum; the adoption
of co-education; the dispersal from Tokyo to the prefectures
of the national universities: the reorganization of teacher
training so thet it is a part of university education or at
least on that level; and the reorganisation of education fer
the professions. These documents are believed to be repri-
sentative of the thinking of several influential Japanese
educational and political leaders.
It is believed that Mr. WATANABE's proposals have much
merit. Because of his lack of influence with officials of
the Education Ministry and of the Government, resulting part-
ly from the fact that he is not a graduate of Imperial Univer-
FILED
sity or of one of the "accepted schools", it is unlikely that
his ideas will have any immediate or direct influence on
02946
official educational reforms and reorganisation. Mr. MATANAB
however, is well acquainted in political circles, especially
2
DCR NE Unit
among
na
Rt
Dar E/y.5

among independent candidates for the Diet. His views are said

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch No. 270 dated February 21, 1946,
from the Office of the United States Political Adviser, Tokyo,
Japan, on the subject "Transmission of Two Statements by
WATANABE Chugo on Educational Reform".
GIST OF PROPOSED REFORM OF EDUCATION
A. Substantial Reform.
1. Renew the historical idea of people by scientific re-
novation in Japenese history, and correct the militaristic
misleading on national polity.
2. Restore the historical beauty and popular satisfaction in
direct harmony and concord between Tenno and people by
scientific clearing of history and by conducting the democra-
tic government - the government of the people, by the
people, for the people - under the rein of Tenno.
3. Social training end its morality should be extremely em-
phasized, avoiding to lean upon the existing family system
end its morelity.
4. Self-respect policy, exclusionism and jingoism should be
wiped out by spread of internationalism and pacifism.
5. Merits an demerits of both capitalism and socialism should
be well balanced in bringing the people back to effort and
labor in order to sustain and revive the new Japan.
6. Root up the cramming system of education attended by the
state textbooks and quick memory test over them, and all the
examination systems existing should be rationally improved.
7. Aim of school education should be taken on moral culture,
studious spirit and physical training (labor discipline
inclusive). The examination methods should cover all the
stated points so as to disdain the makeshift tendency and
to realize the exertion for incessant study.
place
8. Pragmatic education should be taken in/of overemphasized
theoretical education now existing, keeping touch with
practical living of the people.
9. Education of national language should be simplified by
decreasing the use of Chinese letters, wasted time end
toil for which are to be turned to the other scientific
studies.
All the technicel terms should be standardized, libraries
be increased, and laboratories be affiliated.
10. English

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
10. English education should aim ii. practical use, and
be greatly improved both in methods and terms, through
which enlighten the people in culturism, elevating the
people in international knowledge and in fair criticism.
B. Proposed School System.
1. Public School - "KOMIN-GAKKO" - (co-educational system)
should be taken in place of existing national school -
"KOKUMIN-GAKKO", to extend democratic educetion to all
citizens for 8 years compulsorily.
The proposed system holds 16 classes in all, 2 classes
for each of eight years, matriculating 2 times a year
(spring and autumn) according to the birth of pupils.
Skipping one class at a time, 2 years (4 classes)
maximum all through the eight years, should be allowed
for the talented pupil qualified, in order to avoid the
defects of the existing u Torm education.
2. All the middle schools existing should be reorganized
into Middle School (CHUGAkkO) and Girls' Middle School
(JOSHI-CHUGAKKO), both being 3 year system, generaliz-
ing all the subjects in each school, and the specialized
middle school system existing must be abolished.
Subjects in agriculture, commerce and technology shall
be optional according to the unit of the school course
preferred by the desire of the student.
Each middle school holds 6 classes altogether, 2 classes
in each year, enrolling students twice (spring and autumn)
a year without entrance examination in response to the
public school system stated above.
Capacity of school accomodetion will be greatly increased
by the proposed system.
3. Quarter system - spring, summer, autumn and winter
should be taken in the proposed college courses of each
university (co-educational system) open to all the students
according to their qualifications.
Credit of students should be rationally graded in con-
papers end in personal characteristios, in addition to
the written examination of retentive power so far prevail-
ed.
4. All the state universities and colleges existing should
be transferred to each prefectural government according
to their localities situated, and each local government
(Hokkaido being rated as 3 prefectures) is requested to
support 1 to 3 universities in ratio of its population.
Each

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
3
Lach proposed university affiliates the state and
public institutes such as colleges, laboratories
and research stations existing, moving of which are
expected if necessary.
Especially the normal schools and teachers† colleges
must be taken into the proposed university as an
educational course.
Special treatment by way of state or public support
to the students in the said normal schools and
colleges should be abolished.
5. 4 year system shall be taken in each college course
(except 6 to 7 years system in medical course), 2
years in the post-graduate course and 3 years in
the graduate school of each proposed university.
Degree of M.D. should be conferred to all the graduates
in medical course, GAkUSHI (i.e. A.M.) to those who
finished the post-graduate course, and Doctorate to
those qualified in thesis according to their subjects
specialized.
6. Public school teacher, as a rule, must be college
graduats in educational course, middle school teacher
must have qualifications in the post-graduate course,
and professors should be chosen from those who com-
pleted the graduate school or from Doctors, but free
roads from school formality should be always open for
talented persons self-studied.
7. School teachers should be obliged to promote their
studies 3 to 6 months at least for every three years,
taking summer courses in connection with the proposed
university.
College graduates unemployed at present (except the
demobilized officers in Army and Navy) may be adopted
to the special training school for half a year to be
brought up as leaders in pragmatic education and in'
democracy, as a counter plan for the present period.
Existing school teachers should enlighten and elevate
their learning, attending to lectures or taking courses
in the proposed university.
Quite many unqualified teachers are expected to find
their own way out of this educational reform.
Priests and monks in any religion who are taking
additional post in public school should be obliged to
either one.
8. Institutes,

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
8. Institutes, either private or public, in special
professional education, e.g., sewing, cookery,
nursing, language, midwifery, navigation, fishery,
manufacturing of agricultural or aquatic products,
tonsorial arts, crafts, photography, tailoring,
mechanics, etc., etc., should be well equipped and
accommodated under 2 year system at most, and be
strictly supervised according to the middle school
regulations.
9. Executive power regarding selection of textbooks,
superintendencyand administration of public schools,
lies on decision of board of trustees, faculty meeting,
committee meeting of local government with consent of
committee meeting of central government (Monbusho).
School inspectors existing both in local and central
governments should be abolished.
10. The committee meeting of local government should
execute the administrative power on public schools,
middle schools, colleges and universities, with
report to and consent of the central government.
All the school system regulations should be establish-
ed and supervised by the committee meeting of. the
central government.
Board of directors or trustees of each school,
committee meetings of both local and central govern-
ments should be consisted of good reputed able persons,
learned or experienced, elected to represent various
classes of people by democretic means.
11. Most of the private colleges and universities gather-
ed so many in big cities, ere requested to remove to
any locality of their choice, thus preventing the
useless expansion of consuming cities, anddelivering
students from the ill effects caused by peddling lectures
so far prevailed.
Board of trustees of private schools shall be responsible
for the school administration.
School system, appointment or dismissal of teachers and
professors and financial supervision of the same should
have the consent of the committee meeting of the central
government.
12. Pay to the teachers and professors must be greatly in-
creased. They are not to be officialized nor pensioned,
yet their study should be kept free and independent by
all means.
Authorized decisions in meetings of guardians, alumni,
faculties, directors or trustees, committees of local
and central governments, shall be powerful in the
order stated above, and the conference of representatives
from all the meetings above mentioned, takes the supreme
power when necessary.
C. Aims

=== Page 89 === (confidence 1.00)
C. Aims of Reform.
1. Useless classification of elementary course (compulsory
6 years) and higher course (optional 2 years) in the
national school existing, gives an inferior feeling to
those who are destined to remain in the said optional
course, after sending their comrades up to middle schools -
that could be one of the sources that results the survility
and prejudice among farmers and laborers - should be wiped
out by the new scheme.
Majority of the public school pupils will be easily pull-
ed up to the middle schools of 3 year system to receive
the pregmatic (or rather professional) education, even
after their prolonged compulsory course, thus practically
elevating the culture of people as a whole.
2. The gap caused by the disparity of age will be avoided
by the 2 class system for each school year, and the de-
fects in uniform education shall be adjusted by the skipping
system for the pupil of ability, for which a strict and
rational valuation of the candidate should be provided.
 3. The proposed middle school system seems to be graded as
the existing middle school, judging from its school years,
but its standard shall be raised essentially a bit higher
than that by the pragmatic education to be realized in
the substantial reform of subjects and by the practical
rationalization of optional courses, utilizing the time
and toil wasted so far for the military drill and for
tiresome study in Chinese letters.
Practical English shall be required in every courses in
middle school, while the second and third foreign languages
being optional.
4. The high schools, "KOTO-GAKKO", existing, should be
thoroughly abolished as being the cradle of egoistic
bureaucracy and school.fanaticism.
The special situation of the same as the preparatory
school promised to the s tate universities pulls such a
crazy rush to the entrance examination, proudly displayed
as the narrow gate to the highest intellectualism.
5. By being transferred from state to prefecture, universities,
colleges, and all other educational institutes existing,
shall be released from all the nationalistic influences,
that have misused those organs so far as if they are the
training school of officials.
6. Lectures framed in sectionalism of the Department system
in existing universities are inconvenient and wasteful,
while the credit system affords free selection and
opportunity to all the students, enabling them to attend
to famous lectures of reputed professors, that may also
serve to stimulate the lectureship for exertion.
7. Abolition

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
6 -
7. Abolition of entrance examination may assure the
fair play for all concerning the matriculation,
saving the applicants from their sacrificial
agonies. (One should remind the facts of cruelty.
unfairness, and corrupt ways of the entrance
examination so far exercised).
Selections of the students must be strictly performed
in the course of s tudy according to the s tandard re-
quired, to which students are due to accord naturally.
8. Post-graduate courses, graduate schools, extension
divisions of college lectures, scholarships and
fellowships in the proposed system will serve to
offer an equal opportunity to ell talented persons,
and will socialize the academic works with intimate
co-operation between university and society.
Corrupt custom that "graduate from learning" soon
after the school graduation should be eliminated.
9. Degrae of M.D. is apt to be misused and overvalued,
rectification of which is regarded as incurable cancer
for years.
M.D. shall be conferred, therefore, on all the
graduates from medical course, and those who special-
ized their study in the post-graduate courses or in the
graduate school among M.D.holders, shall be awarded with
some other degree according to their study specialized.
10. Reform of normal school system has been a long pending
consideration. Lifelong study and culture, necessary
to activity of all the school teachers, should be
systematized by the proposed plan, by which the roads
shall be open to the talents, aiming a powerful revival
in educational circles.
11. So far as the appointment and dismissal of school
teachers and professors are left to be controlled by
such a minority as school inspectors and officials
existing, dark shadow will always follow into corrup-
tion.
Committee meetings, under the representative system
proposed, should lead the authority to fair personnel
administration and righteous judgement to protect the
freedom and independence of learning.
12. Professional specification in middle schools existing,
that forces the youngsters to their early decision in
selecting their life work, is not reasonable in any way.
The generalization system proposed will solve the fault
stated above, and will desolve the cruel barrier of
entrance examination by increased accommodations and
attending facilities.
13. The

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.99)
13. The fact that about 50 prefectural universities are
supposed to be established by this reform plan may seem
to be radical, but on the contrary it is an unification
and even simplification of existing colleges, normal
schools, experimental stations and research laboratories,
which are left in the hand of wasteful bureaucracy -
numbering 7 universities, 160 colleges, 102 normal
schools and many other doubled institutes.
Such small number of state university and separatism in
colleges and normal schools existing, yielded the
academic cliquism and favouritism, that have corrupted
the social circumstances in such a bottomless depth.
It is only way possible and inevitable to organize the
existing school system along the route stated. Those
proposed universities shall be the original centers of
peaceful civilization, democratic education and industrial
promotion in and for each localities where they are.
14. Proposed universities will result the economic gains in
better disposal of personnel, in facilities and efficiency,
and in common utilizing of all e quipments packed in the
old castles of separatism.
15. Unfairness and bribery so far prevailed in professional
colleges and schools, especially in private institutes,
authorized to confer the state licenses, e.g., medical
license, dental license, pharmaceuticel license, etc.,
will be expelled naturally by the proposed system -
increased capacity of school accomodations, local faculty
in attending schools, and no entrance examination anywhere,
but with strict supervision upon school discipline.
(By Chugo Watanate, former Director of Japan Trade
Promotion Association, Tokyo.)

Enclosure No. 2 to despatch No. 270 dated Jebruary 21, 1946,
from the Office of the United States Political Adviser, Tokyo,
Japan, on the subject ""Transmission of Two Statements by
WATANÁBE Chugo on Educational Reform".
PRACTICAL APPLICATION OF THE EDUCATIONAL
REFORM PLAN
A. School System.
1. All the "Imperial Universities" are the cradles of national-
ism, the painting sprays of self-respectism, and the strong-
holds of bureaucracy.
Especially, the "Tokyo Imperial University" is the most re-
presentative one, famous in exclusive production of officials,
who are so much prerogative to be pulled into the field of
academic cliquism and favoritism as almost all the important
positions in the Japanese government. are under their occupa
tion and control.
The headquarters of education - Monbusho - is also an im-
pregnable fortification of those graduated from the Tokyo
Imperial University and the Tokyo University of Science and
Literature (the post-graduate course of the Tokyo Higher
Normal School).
They are still holding the two despotic weapons, called
"school inspector system" and "book recommendation system",
both of which are utilized only by and for the corrupt
officials, in controlling the schools and teacher on one
side, and the publishers and editors on the other.
This educational jungle is so much wide spread and deep
rooted that any of the administrative powers, especially
the ones that may come out in future by so- alled democratic
election, will be unable to clear up them by being tangled
in their dark nets.
But just one stroke right now is sure to clear up the en-
tanglement, with easiness and speed, to the ends of the
corrupt roots.
It is really regretful that the power for the stroke can
be obtained only from outside of the Japanese government,
as it is netted with the said "Imperial University" webs.
Just one arrow now, not later, and only this arrow, shall
be able to shoot down the mark named the "Imperial University".
which is the nest of nationalism and bureaucracy, the origin
of academic discrimination and factionalism, and the mother
of official superiority and private inferiority.
If this great HIT could be done, the rest of the difficulties
in the educational reform shall be easily adjusted with eager
and

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and eternal applause of the thankful peoples.
2. Any of those proposed schools, colleges and universities
should not be allowed to use the term that indicates
national, regional or prefectural distinctions, e.g.,
"Imperial", Nippon, Tokyo, Osaka, Kyoto, Hokkaido, Tohoku,
Kanto, Kansai, Kyushu, Koa, etc., crowned upon the name
of the educationel institutes.
Schools, colleges a universities should not be legalized
by crowning the term that indicates the school discrimina-
tions, either governmental or private.
The names of town or village where the school exists, the
name of the person in honor of his service to the school,
or other proper names, are recommendable instead of the
above stated.
3. The University of Science and Literature, the Higher Normal
School, and the Normal School - all of these shall be
amalgamated in the educational course of the proposed pre
fectural university, by which the enmities and intrigues
among the colorings of the Imperial University and the
University of Science and Literature (Normal School), will
be faded away.

those students left by the wor dead, should be rectified from

15. In sending the professors abroad for their s tudy so far, pulls
and favors in and from the Department of Education - Monbusho -
seemed to be most powerful in acquiring the privilege, disregard-
ing their personal qualifications nor necessities.
Consequently, quite a number of them were sent only to enjoy the

Such national expense should be turned to and for the young

and middle school teachers, either personally or in groups, for
which the School Committee System in recommendation of scholars
should be adopted in the place of the corrupt School Inspector
System existing.
16. The following four great corrupt systems should be abolished:
(1) School Inspector System:
So far as the appointment and dismissal, transfer and
interchange, and various other favors or privileges in
personnel administration are left to be handled by the
report of school inspectors, the position of inspector
is something like the human broker or go-between, who
are so busy in seeking around their brokerages and
commissions.
The commission merchants as such in the educational
trade between teechers and fficials should be duely
abolished.
(2) Book Recommendation System:
Only the books published by some powerful publishers are
easy to be recommended in the name of the central govern-
ment - the Department of Education. Only the books written
by influential militerists or by sagacious scholars of
"Imperial

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
"Imperial University" are apt to be easily recommend-
ed in the name of the Educational Headquarters.
There is no reason in thet the publisher, who wishes
to make a good seller, is always careless in respect
to the advertising velue of the "national recommenda-
tion".
No more additional narration shall be necessitated in
order to have such corrupt system abclished.
(3) Supporting System:
The national and prefectural supporting system to the
students in normal schools and colleges is derived from
the Normal School Act, 1886, provided to bring up the
talents from the poor farmers who were destined not to
be the heir under the family system.
But the present circumstances are entirely different
from the same of sixty years ago. Furthermore, the said
system served only for resulting the servitude and
obedient tendency of the talents to be easily controlled
by the nationelistic powers, their freedom being restrain-
ed by their obligations in the due course of returning the
money.
All the supporting system, therefore, should not be establish-
ed upon the nationel nor prefectural, in other words, govern
mental basis.
(4) Pension System:
The pension system for the teachers and professors only in
governmental schools, and not in private schools, is also an
unfair treatment, but is very convenient way in keeping them
in the frame of nationalism under the management of bureau
crats.
Teachers and professors should be insured by the insurance
company not compulsorily but by their free will, for which
salaries for them should be greatly increased, and in which
the talents shall find their freedom and satisfaction to be
diligent in their duty and study.
17. Regarding the textbooks, the Department of Education - Monbusho -
should present only outlines of principles to be taken in the
books, namely - democracy, internationalism, pacifism, liberalism,
pragmatism, culturism, scientism, anti-pedentism, sacredness of
labor, loyalism by and through socialism, etc., so as to stimulate
the excellent writers into incessant activities for the work.
Spoiled bureaucrats wish to grasp everything in their hands. The
unification of textbooks by state is nothing but the product of
nationalism, and the publication of the state textbooks is not
for the pupil, but only for the corrupt way of control.
The selection and adoption of the democratic textbooks, there
fore, should be managed by the school committees with the consent
of the committees of prefectural unit, to which none of the
publishers shall find the room for their profitable movements.

B. Social Application.
1. The bottom current of self-respectism, rationalism and jingoism,
overflown into the masses, runs in the dreadful reciting of
old story-telling (kodan), and Naniwabushi (passionate narra
tion of story in singing style), together with the tremendous
publications of the same as well as cheap novels.
These low-grade literatures, made up to propagate the slavish
morels as if prevailed among the heroic loyalist and the
chivalrous boss-dicers in the background of the Meiji Restora-
tion Era, pull the minds of vulgars and ignorants so much as
thet they are absorbed passionately in reading them even in
the overloaded street-cars so terible es at present.
These are really said to be the hidden weapons of militarism
end jingoism, of which the Dainippon Yubenkai Kodansha (The
Eloquence and Story-telling Society of Great Japan, Ltd.), is
the most representetive publisher.
2. Another influential journalism, tied up with the military
circles at the beginning of the war, that stirred up the senti-
ment of the people is the Yomiuri-Hochi, a daily news.
As socn as the war is over, they took initiative to turn into
the communistic movement, driving out the head as the erasing
sanctifier for the executive staffs to be responsible in any
way together with their head.
Both of the above-mentioned journalism are really said to be
the dynamic motcrs of social evils in and national faults of
Japan, which can hardly be dispelled by any authority in
Japanese hand now, as the journalism, either in good or bad
sense, is so powerful especially in peace time of so-called
"Democratic Age", not in its real sense.

the adult education, social training, and democratic discipline
can be obtained if lead in the right away.
Corresponding to the above, the proposed prefectural university
system and the extension divisior of the college and university
courses shell find the way to their incessant social activities
for edult education and local culture. Moreover, professors of
colleges and universities shall be sent for lectures and guid-
ances in co-operetion with the central and local governments,
providing that the corrupt expenses, so far and so much wasted
by the YOKUSANKAI (Patriots' Society) members in drinking and
eating party, formelly given to the lecturers, only to lay out
the budget for the official themselves, should be absolutely
rectified.
(By Chugo Watanabe, former Director of Japan Trade Promotion
Association, Tokyo.)

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
