Press Release:
18 February 1946
PLAN TO EVADE FISHING RESTRICTIONS BLOCKED
Another fantastic Japanese scheme to break out of the restricted
fishing area -- this time under the protection of the American flag,
2
using American ships, equipment and materials -- has been spiked,
f
according to Lt. Col. R. H. Fiedler, fisheries expert of the Natural
2
Resources Section, GHQ, SCAP. The request, which was not presented
through official channels, was immediately denied.
"The significance of this scheme is not the mere fact that it is
just another attempt to break out of the fishing areas now open to
Japan, but the audacious manner in which the Japanese firm presumes
to use the United States and the American flag to accomplish its
ends," Col. Fiedler said.
Since the occupation, the Japanese fishing industry has been
permitted to operate in an area around Japan which, on the basis of
past production records, is considered adequate to supply the food
needs of the Japanese people. Since the surrender the Japanese have
made repeated attempts to extend their fishing grounds (see PRo re-
lease 13:30, 25 January 1946) in an attempt to salavage a part of
their pre-war commercial fishing empire and to reestablish themselves
in the world markets.
"This plan, presented by a private concern to SCAP, suggests that
the firm furnish Japanese crews to operate American ships flying the
American flag so that they could invade American fishing areas with
impunity," Col. Fiedler said. "They then propose to can crab meat on
board the ships and to.deliver it directly to the American market,
where, because it comes from American ships, it would become a product
of the American fisheries and enter the country duty free."
Col. Fiedler branded the proposal as a brazen attempt to use the
American flag and United States resources to evade the restrictions
placed upon the Japanese fishing industry after the surrender and to
help recapture the world fishing empire which Japan enjoyed before

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
the war. He pointed out that the plan not cnly proposed to invade
American crab fishing ground but also looked toward the exploitation
of estern Pacific salmon fishing areas under the protection of the
American flag.
The plan was presented by a pre-war Japanese-American firm of
Tokyo, which submitted a request directly to SCAP without the usual
clearance with Central Liaison asking that they be permitted to "char-
ter, lease or otherwise obtain a few ships, cans, nets and all other
equipment necessary for crab cannery ships." The firm would send the
vessels to Hakodate for conversion to crab cannery equipment and
enlist Japanese crews to operate them.
"The cannery ships, upon being ccnverted at Hakodate, would leave
for the fishing grounds flying the American flag," the plan states.
"And as all the fishing grcunds are located outside the territorial
waters, no trouble of any kind is anticipated." (The ships would be
under the protection of the Am rican flag.)
The scheme proposes that the United States furnish ten cargo
boats of from 3,000 to 4,0oo tons each. The Japanese firm would
furnish 3,500 veteran Japanese crab fishermen. By the end of September
they would expect to produce 20o,0o0 cases of canned crab eat having
a value of approximately l0o million yen, all of which would be
shipped to America.
"Moreover, the production of canned salmon had been carried on
by Japanese off the Russian coast," the plan adds. "This, too, could
be resumed under a system similar to that proposed for crab canning.
If this were to be undertaken also, there wculd result an additional
yield of another 1o0 million yen." The plan suggests that some of the
products might be used for reparations.
The presentation opens with an elaborate introduction which is
designed to emphasize the sincerity with which the Japanese are attempt-
ing to solve their post-war problems. It recognizes the importance
of America's sympathy "in this trying period of our country's transitioi
from warlike militarism to peaceful democracy." It appeals to gourmets
by describing the crab meat products which will be restored "to the
tables of the people the world over who have been waiting to taste

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
this delicacy once more" and it solicits sympathy for the "large
numbers of fishermen of high skill and long experience in the produc-
tion of canned crabs, all of them whom much to our regret, are now
spending their days in idleness for want of the work to which they
can apply their attributed."
-0-
JAPANESE GOVERNL ENT NOT TO PRESCRIBE BOOKS
In one of what is expected to be a series of moves toward de-
centralization of control over education in Japan, the Ministry of
Education has abandoned its former "right" to authorize text books
for higher schools.
The Ministry informed SCAP's Civil Information and Education
Section that in the future the c hoice of books will be left to the
higher schools concerned.
The section also was informed that publishers of text books for
higher schools have formed an association to insure equitable dist-
ribution of publishing rights.
-0-
JAPANESE EDUCATION BEING LIBERALIZED
"The Japanese educational system is frccing itself of the
insidious pcison of militarism and ultra-nationalism and is moving
into an era of democratic reform," stated Brig. Gen. Ken R. Dyke,
chief of the Civil Information and ducation Section of SCAP, in a
four-month summarization report released today by his office.
General
Dyke explained that fqur thousand schools were totally
or partially destroyed during the war and existing facilities are
dangerously overcrowded. However, in the emergency, other public
buildings and temples are being utilized and many schools are operat-
ing double shifts to share buildings with other institutions.
The report said that most of Japan's 18 illion students and 400
thousand teachers are at work in some 40 thousand schools.
(more)
-3-

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
The Education Division of the Civil Information and Education
Section, SCAP, was established to make "recommendations to effect the
accomplishment of the educational objectives of the Allied Powers."
To this end, the division has been working through the revamped Japan-
ese Ministry of Education, using supervisory techniques which have
combined technical advice and assistance with formal direction and
official sanction.
For the first time in Japanese educational history both the min-
ister and vice-minister of education of the recently reorganized cab-
inet are professional educators.
The basic education policies and objectives of the occupation,
promulgated in an October 22 directive, have given the Japanese "a fresh
start and a new kind of hope," the general said. In this directive a
long-range program f educational reform was predicated on both neg-
ative and positive aspects of Japanese rericntation.
"On. the one hand," the report stated, "it prohibitcd the dissem-
ination by the Japanese Government of all militaristic and ultra-nat-
ionalistic ideology; and on the other hand. the Government, through its
Education Ministry, was directed to encourage 'the inculcation of con-
cepts and establishment of practices in harmony with representative
government, international pcace, the dignity of the individual, and
such fundamental human rights as the frcedom of assembly, speech and
religion.'"
These fundamental policies wcre established to govern the careful
cxamination of teachers and educatinal officials, school curricula,
textbooks, teaching manuals and instructional materials.
Since the occupation began, military cducation and drill have
been prohibited. Personnel who could not mect the new standards were
ordered removed and objcctionablc material deleted from all teaching
matter. Discrimination against students, tcachers and educational
officials on grounds of race, nationality, creed, political opinion
r social position was forbidden.
(more)
-4

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
operating educational system as soon as possible. Teachers and
educational officials who had been suspended or forced to resign
for liberal or anti-militaristic opinions or activities were declared
immediately eligible for reappointment, providing their technical
qualifications were acceptable.
Students, teachers and educational officials were encouraged to
evaluate critically and intelligently the content of instruction and
to engage in free and unrestricted discussion of issues involving
political, civil and religious liberties.
New curricula, textbooks, teaching manuals and instructional
materials designed to produce an educated, peaceful and responsible
citizenry, the report stated, are in the process of preparation to be
substituted for militaristic and ultra-nationalistic content.
Gone since the end cf the war is martial training in the schools,
including kendo (fencing), judo (military wrestling) and naginata
(girls' fencing). Military subjects, such as training in aeronautics,
have been discontinued. Strict military discipline has ceased and
where facilities and equipment are available, students are engaging
i free sports such as basketbell, volleyball and track.
Occasional violations of the directive banning military training
have been reported and necessary action has been taken, but in general,
there has been an apparent attempt to comply with the provisions of
the directive, according to the report.
Gencral Dyke explained that most school playgrounds, of necessity,
have been turned into vegetable gardens and that all schools are almost
totally lacking in athletic equ:prcnt. Recognizirg the importance of
sports and games in any program designed to democratize the school
system, SCAP is drawing up plans to meet many of the problems.
Former students of naval and military schools, now closed, have
been distributed among other institutions in order that they will not
represent more than ten percent of the student body at any one school.
(more)
-5-

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
Many old-line teachers and officials, in recognition of their
own militaristic and ultra-nationalistic activities, have resigned
voluntarily and others have been removed. To further cleanse the
school system of und sirablc influences, the Japanese Ministry of
Education is preparing a plan by which all of Japan's 400 thousand
teachers will bc examined individually--a tremendous and far-reaching
project.
Teachers and cducational officials who were suspended or forced
to resign for libcral opinions during thc war years, if otherwise
qualified, are being given preference in reappointment. Liberal teach
crs, General Dykc pointed out, are lcarning that they are needed and
that places will be made for them.
"Long deprived of information concerning the educational advances
of democratic countries, teachers and students of Japan now are being
reoricnted by special publications and lectures, radio and motion
pictures, magazines and newspapers," the report stated. "While it is
recognized that this orientation is limited in its effect, it can be
considered an important step in the overall program."
A ncw teachers' manual is being prepared by the Ministry of Ed-
ucation to replace the vicious, interpretativ. guidcbooks which were
a primary source of war-timc indoctrination. Traditionally, Japanese
teachers relied on thesc manuals for interpretation of textbook con-
tent and for teaching aids. The ncw version is intended to set forth
the basic aims which tuachers may use in re-thinking thc aims of ed-
ucation and the function of schools and to illustrate spccific learn-
ing activitics.
Japanesc teaching manuals always have becn pcdantic and abstruse
and frequently supplcmentary publications were necessary to explain
them to thc tcachers.
. The new guides will be prescnted in colloquial
Japanese which the average clementary tcacher can comprchend.
The Ministry of Education, on its wn initiative, has launched
a serics of teacher re-orientation lectures throughout Japan to inter-
pret the new cducational policies. These will be helpful, but not
adequate, the general said.
(more)

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
Radio broadcasting to the schools has been resumed by the Ministry
of Education and new programs ere being conducted especially for the
interim period while school texts are inadequate or unavailable. The
Radio and Education Divisions of CI&E are cooperating to provide
technical advice and assure appropriate material for the broadcasts.
Radio programs designed to stimulatc discussion of the practices
and meaning of democracy, and the problems of student and teacher
readjustment have taken the form of lectures, commentaries, open
forums, dramatic skits, stories and newscasts.
A weekly discussion of current events, in which students
participate, is broadcast as a part of the daily Students' Hour.
Teachers of elementary, middle and youth schools are encouraged to
use this program as a model for classroom discussion.
"While it is hoped that education by radio will be helpful in
the democratization process," the report said, "it must be remembered
thi: the majority of Jananesc teachers and students will not be
reached for sore time to come. A recent Ministry of Education survey
indicates that less than one-third of the elemntary and middle schools
 cquipped with opercble radio sets. Many of ties. do not have
corplete public address systers with loud speakers in cach classroom.
Schools will get first priority on new radio sets produced in Japan."
The Ministry has announced long-range plans for raising the level
of women's @ducation "to cope with the realization of women's part-
icipation in politics."
Traditionally, Goneral Dyke said, the education of girls and wc-
men in Japan has been based on the idea that they become good wives
and mothers. There has been no widespread belief that women should
be trained in academic subjects or for c career other than motherhood.
Under the new plan, women's education will be reformed with a
view to "giving equal opportunity of receiving cducation to men and
women; raising the level of women's education to the level of men's
education, and furthering mutual respect among men and women."
Specifically, this will mean admitting women to schools formerly
restricted to men; putting coeducation into operation in the univ-
ersities and in addition it will mean the problem of

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.99)
establishing women's universities; establishing women's higher
schools, and raising the level of the curriculum to that of men's
institutions.
Textbooks in Morals, Geography and Japanese History were found
to be so filled with militaristic and ultra-nationalistic ideology
that it was neccssary to suspend the courses. As a temporary measure,
the Ministry of Education was directed to prepare a substitute pro-
gram to rcplace these courses.
"The present school texts, in the main, are free of a greater
part of the objectionable war-time material," the general said. "Up
to the present, 188 of the 497 school texts, from elementary through
the normal school level, have been carefully censored by SCAP."
All other texts are being examined as rapidly as thetremendously
involved problems of translation and review will permit. General
criteria for censorship have been cstablished for the elimination of
militaristic and ultra-nationalistic content. Thcsc books will be
used temporarily, pending the preparation of entirely new texts on the
basis of detailcd and spccific criteria.
The Japancsc Ministry of Education has appointed a committce of
cducators to coopcrate vwith the mission f Amcrican educators which
will come to Japan to study thc cducational system and advise General
MacArthur on matters of policy and long-range objectives. After the
dcparture of the American mission, the Japanesc committee will contin-
ue to serve tho inistry as an advisory body on educational reform.
The Amcrican mission, in particular, will study Education for
Democracy in Japan, Psychology in thc Rc-cducation of Japan, Admin-
istrative Rcorganization of the Educational System, and Higher Educa-
tion in the Rehabilitation of Japan.
Spccializcd committecs of Japanese, representing a cross-section
of educational, professional and civic-mindcd citizens will be asked
to work with thc American mission independently free of Ministry of
Education instructions or influences.
"The great task of educational reform in Japan," General Dyke
concluded," has only just begun. A tentative blueprint has been laid
-8

down but the careful, painstaking process of carrying out the pro-
visions of Potsdam lies chead. It will not be an easy undertaking."

SEOUL, KOREA -- Threc hundred and thirty Koreans, the first to

with former experience in thc field.

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to despatch No. 375 dated April 16, 1946, from the
United States Political Adviser, Tokyo, Japan, on the subject
"Attitude of Leading Japanese Political Parties Toward Educa-
tion".
COPY
MEMORANDUM
March 27, 1946
SUBJECT: Attitude of Political Parties Toward Education
The platforms of the political parties contain little
specific material on educational problems even in their ex-
pressed attitudes on ideology and social conditions.
The following statements have been taken from the party
platforms.
Progressive Party
1. Improvement of netional education based on personal-
ity development and spirit of service. A statement on the edu-
cation policies of the Party wes issued February 16, 1946, and
is attached as an appendix.
Liberal Party
1. Abolition of narrow nationelistic ideology: establish-
ment of Japanese-type democrecy based on the principle "Emperor
and People in one".
2. Establishment of national cultural institute.
3. Reform of laws for universities.
4. Extend school terms to 3 years for high schoo, 3 years
for university, and 5 years for middle school.
5. Abolition of standardized compulsory education. En-
couragement of individuality of students through respect accorded
to individual opinions of instructors.
6. Encouragement of social science: development of civic
knowledge and civic virtues.
7. Establishment of complete equality between state and
private schools: abolition of academic cliques (gakubatsu).
8. Improvement of prestige and material treatment of teachers.
9. Abolition of Government influence in religion and arts.
10. Encouragement of competitive sports.
Social Democratic Party
1. Developmentof a new Japanese national culture and free
exchange of international culture.
2. Promotion of a spirit of social solidarity based on re-
spect for the individual.
3. Diffusion of knowledge of socialism; unification of labor
and education.
4. Elevation of the national level of scientific and techni-
cal knowledge.
5. Promotion of scientific technology and establishment of
research organs; improvement in treatment of scientists and tech-
nicians.
6. Adoption of the metric system.
7. Extension of the period of compulsory education; thorough
reorganization of the educational system.
8. Government scholarships to deserving students without
financial resources.
9. Elimination

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
2
9. Elimination of Government interference in religion.
10. Respect and guarantee of freedom for the arts.
1l. Improvement of physique of people through encouragement
of sports.
12. Promotion of Esperanto as an international language.
Communist Party
1. Opposition to militaristic, imperialistic legislation,
culture, and education. Establishment and vigorous dissemination
of progressive culture for the emancipation of the people.
These statements show that some party leaders realize that
educational reform and increased financial support of education
must be faced in the next Diet. There is agreement that educe-
tion must be democratized, that the cirriculum must be revised,
that civic education is necessary, that teachers' salaries must
be increased, and that the school term must be lengthened. There
is some support for increasing the number of years of compulsory
education, for the decentralization of school authority in order
to break up the concentration of higher educational institutions
around Tokyo, for the acceptance by the Diet of responsibility
for education, and for establishing legal equality between state
and private institutions.
The promotion of scientific education is also singled out
for attention. Many Japanese feel that the war was lost because
the Japanese were insufficiently educated in science to compete
on an equal basis with America and Great Britain.
The Progressive Party was the first to advocate that any
laws fundamental to education should be enected in the future by
the Diet, in lieu of Imperial ordinances.
Although the party leaders recognize that political educa-
tion is advisable, if not necessary, for the newly enfranchised
women and for youths who have not previously voted, the campaign
has not developed much educational activity. Political leaders
have arranged for educational talks for women and for youths by
party workers and by teachers, who have emphasized the duty to
vote and discussed the economic stituation. Many articles have
been published in the metropolitan and provincial papers directed
toward the political education of women.
There has not been much political activity in colleges or
universities. Many institutions at first did not permit the for-
mation of political clubs. At most of these institutions students
and faculty have made arrangements for candidates and officers
of the political parties to speak to faculty and students. Fre-
quently student groups initiate these arrangements through their
economics or literary societies.
The attitude of the Ministry of Education regarding the
participation of teachers and students in politics was clarified
somewhat about the end of February when it was announced that
there was no objection in principle to participation of faculty
and students in the political gampaigns. Permitted activities
included the formation of political groups, and students and
teachers were to be allowed to become candidates. Such activities,
it was

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
-3~
it was ruled, must not interfere with university courses. Party
propaganda must not be carried on in the classes. Otherwise there
were no restrictions. In accordance with Article 10 of the Elee-
tion Law, a teacher in e government school must resign his post
if elected to a public office. This provision does not apply to
teachers in private institutions. In the past, several private
college and university teachers have held political elective
offices and retained their institutional position and salary.
It was to be anticipated that following the above decision
the political parties would proceed to organize political clubs
among college and university students. Although there are a few
such instances, in general little has been accomplished. The
Communist Party carries on student work through the Student League.
The Jiji Press , March 7, 1946, carried a stody that the Communist
Party was planning to organize university and college students inte
Kakushin Gakuto Domei (Reformist League of Students) under the
leadership of SATo Yoshino, a professor at Doshisha University.
The Progressive Party announced a plan to organise student clubs,
which met with little or no success.
An editorial in Minami Nippon Shimbun (Kagoshima), February
10, 1946, stated that "local leaders of the Socialist Party de
clare that...utterances of some of the primary school principals
are palpable interference with the party's election campaigm".
The paper reported that the Socialist leaders are collecting evi-
dence to campaign against such educators on the ground that their
attitude is *...conducive to the spread of a reactionary trend of
thought." Another editorial in this paper demanded "an epochal
personnel reshuffling" in the educational circles of the prefecture.
The political campaign has influenced the teachers: unions,
both nationally and locally. There is little evidence that any
political party has great influence over these unions, although
there have been statements to the effect that the Communist and
Socialists are more influential than the other parties. There is
evidence to show that the parties do not actually control the
unions, but members of all political parties are active in the
unions. There have been a number of reports of local union
officials taking precautions to prevent the untons from being
used as tools in the political campaign.
The task of the political parties in this current campaign
is immense. Food, economic, and constitutional problems are so
impertant that political parties, not being sure of the student
minority vote, have spent most of their time, money, and organizing
effort with the adult voters and on the turning out of the vote of
all eligible to vote. They have not devoted especial attention to
the problems of education or to students or teachers as groups.
Political leaders are anxious to have a high percentage of
votes cast in order to prove that the Japanese people are capable
of organizing a democratic government.
Political party statements and activities regarding educa-
tion show thet education is not to be considered a very important
issue in this campaign. Little attention has been given in cam
paign speeches to the educational system. The party platforms con-
tain a few generalities on the democratization of education, the
improvement of conditions for teachers, the elimination of mili-
tarism, and the changing of teaching methods to develop the in-
dividuality of students. The more specific statement of the
Progressive Party (appendix) advocates that all laws regarding
education be passed by the Diet in the future, thus abandoning
the system of Imperial Rescripts. It also recommends a complete
reform

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.96)
4
of the sducational system, and the rehabilitation of the war
damagec schools on a national planning basis which would de-
centralise institutions.
The Communist leader, NOSAka Sanzo, is quoted by the Nippen
Times, April 2, 1946, as saying in reply to the question "What
would your policy be in education?", "Our educational policy would
be according to our general ideology". Again in response to the
question "If a religious school were regarded as hostile to
Communism, we could not allow it."
This study of the attitude of political parties toward
education reveals that little can be learned from their published
and spoken statements as to what they intend to do as a party
regarding educational legislation. Few commitments or promises
have been made.
Karl C. Leebrick
842
KCLeebrick: jwb

Washington, D. C.
John Edgar Hoover - Director, Federal Bureau of Investigation
Subject:
ALLEGED COMMUNIST INFLUENCE IN
SELECTION OF EDUCATORS FOR JAPAN
I thought the Secretary and you would be interested in some
information recently reaching our Chicago Office through a confidential
source.
According to the source, in connection with the compiling of
the list of educators to be sent to Japan, it was felt by the American
CS/RH
Federation of Teachers, an American Federation of Labor affiliate, that
a representative member of their organization had been omitted deliberately.
This list was reported to have been composed by the Cultural Relations
Staff in the State Department under the direction of Charles Thompson.
It was the opinion of the informant that Communist influence in
the State Department was responsible for the omission of a representative
of the American Federation of Teachers from the list. He reported that a
man named Jacobson whose first name is unknown but who was formerly
connected with the University of Chicago, is now employed by the State
Department and reputedly possesses Communistic leanings. He also advised
that Kermit Eby, Educational Director of the CIO, whom he considers a
Communist Party sympathizer, is on the State Department's approved list
of educators to be sent to Japan.

The foregoing is furnished you merely as a matter of information.

SUBJECT:
Appointment of liaison officers for information and cultural
affairs to SCAP and AMGIK
895.42
As you know, on or about July lst OIC personnel in occupied
areas will be transferred to the War Department peyrolls. Simul-
taneously, CAD will become responsible for servicing inter alia
the Civil Information and Education Section of SCAP and AMGIK.
It is hoped that arrangements can be worked out to provide suitable
liaison between OIC and the Reorientation Branch of CAD. In order
to insure, however, that the Department's long-range views on day
to day questions in the field of information and cultural affairs
can be brought to the attention of SCAP and AMGIK, and having in
mind the eventual transfer to the Department of the responsibility
for implementation in these fields, I feel very strongly that a
Departmental liaison officer in the field of information and cultural
894.42/6-2146
affairs should be immediately assigned to SCAP and to AMGIK. There
would appear to be two ways in which this could be done:
a. an officer from OIC could be assigned to the Diplomatic
Section of SCAP and to the Political Adviser's office
in Seoul, respectively;
b. an officer from OIC could be lent to SCAP and AMGIK
to work in the departments of Civil Information and
Education, and Bureau of EducationPublic Information
Section respectively.
It is believed that in the case of Korea it would be preferable
to assign someone to POLAD and in the case of Tokyo, where there is
no longer a POLAD, to assign a man directly to C I & E.
In either case it would be contemplated that officers would
be sent for a period of from 3 to 6 months in order that the thinkingΩ
of SCAP and AMGIK on information and cultural matters could be 
S
frequently made available to the Department in a personal manner 
In both cases the officers would be integral members of the respocti?
headquarters and would not report to the Department except insofar

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 840, dated January 29, 1947, from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the subject
"Request by Soviet Member of Allied Council for Japan for Information
Concerning Japanese Education".
TRANSLATION
COPY
ALLIED COUNCIL FOR JAPAN
TOKYO
OFFICE OF THE MEMBER
FOR
THE UNION OF SOVIET SOCIALIST REPUBLICS
To: Chief of the Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters
Ambassador George Atcheson
Mr. Ambassador:
In connection with your comment at the allied Council on
13 November 1946 that General Headquarters is making a study of the
question of "decentralization of Japanese system of education", as
well as that the question of reorganization of the education system
in Japan is being developed by the Japanese Government, I have the
honor to request you that information be given me concerning the
following questions:
1. A copy of the English translation of the project of reorgan-
ization of system of education in Japan, developed by the Japanese
Government, submitted to General Headquarters.
2. What directions were given by General Headquarters to the
Japanese Government on the questions concerning public education,
during the period from April 1946 up to the present time.
3. Headquarters' material on the decentralization of the
Japanese system of education.
4. The proposed plan of publishing new manuals for the Japanese
schools and also, if possible, one copy of each of the new manuals
already published.
5. In what stage of their accomplishment are the proposals made
by the Mission of American Educators who visited Japan in March 1946.
6. The list of members of the Central Commission on Public Edu-
cation, of the Japanese Ministry of Education.
Respectfully yours,
/s/ K. Derevyanko
Lieutenant General
Member, Allied Council for Japan
from U.S.S.R.
No. 1011
10 Jan. 1947

=== Page 149 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 840, dated January 29, 1947, from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the subject
"Request by Soviet Member of Allied Council for Japan for Information
Concerning Japanese Education".
COPY
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE AILIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
23 January 1947
Dear General Derevyanko:
Reference is made to your 1etter no. 1011, 10 January 1947, on
the general subject of the reorganization and decentralization of
the Japanese system of education. With reference to the several
questions raised in your letter under reply, the following information
is set forth.
1. A copy of an English translation of the Interim
Report of the Japanese Education Reform Council, including
all recommendations made up to and including the meeting
of December 27, 1946, is enclosed. A number of the basic
recommendations contained in this report are now being
implemented by the Ministry of Education. Such implemen-
tation will also include new educational legislation.
2. Copies of memoranda issued by the Supreme Commander
to the Japanese Government on educational matters since
April 1, 1946 are enclosed. Reforms in Japanese education
are being developed by the Ministry of Education under the
supervision of General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers. Planning and progress of the Ministry
of Education is continuing.
3. The report of the United States Education Mission
which visited Japan during March 1946 made recommendations
regarding decentralization of the administrative control of
the Japanese educational system. The recommendations re-
garding decentralization of administrative control, in general,
have formed the basic policy adopted by this Headquarters.
4. A new course of study, including instructions to
teachers, and textbooks for the revised curricula are being
prepared by the Ministry of Education. Copies will in due
course be available to the Members of the Council.
5. A number of the recommendations of the United States
Education Mission regarding instruction material are being
implemented as described in paragraph 4. Ministry of Education
plans for implementation of other recommendations of the United
States Education Mission are included in the implementation of
the reconmendations of the Japanese Education Reform Council as
mentioned in paragraph 1.
6. A
Lieutenant General K. N. Derevyanko,
Member for the Union of
Soviet Socialist Republics,
Allied Council for Japan,
Tokyo.

III. THE AIM OF EDUCATION AND THE BASIC LAW OF EDUCATION
(Adopted at the General Meeting, 29 November 1946)
1. The need for enacting a basic law of education was recognized.
2. The aim of education should be stated in the basic law of
education as follows:
a. Object of Education:
The object of education shall be the enlightenment
of human nature and the development of a nation, sound in mind and
body, - a democratic and peaceful society and state composed of
individuals who are dedicated to the principles of truth, justice,
and cooperation, and who value personality and who respect the
dignity of labor.
b. Principle of Realization of Education:
For realization of this object of education, efforts
should be devoted to nuturing through education an autonomous and
spontaneous spirit within each individual to enable him to contribute
to the creation and development of culture through his respect and
affection for and collaboration with others.
c. The Basic Law:
The above paragraphs, a and b, should be attached to
the basic law of education in order to make clear the origin and
purport of that law. The contents of the basic law should make clear
the following points:
(1) Defects of the uniform and perfunctory education
as previously conducted.
(2) That the construction of a new democratic cultural
state in harmony with the new Constitution depends
upon the strength of education. This law should
show the trend of the new concept of education.
(3) Relations between the basic education law and the
new Constitution and between the law and other
educational regulations.
(4) That the reform of Japanese education requires
resolution on the part of the Nation.
d. Provisions of the Basic Law:
The basic law of education should define the educational
implications of the new constitution as regards the following items,
and should point out clearly the fundamental principle of each item:
(1) Equality

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's 840,
January 29, 1947.
-6-
(1)
Equality of opportunity in education.
(2)
Compulsory education.
(3)
Education of girls and women.
(4)
Social education.
(5)
Political education.
(6)
Religious education.
(7)
Intrinsic nature of the school.
(8)
Status of teachers.
(9)
Educational administration.
e. Scope of Basic Law:
In drafting the basic law of education, consideration
should be given to deliberations of the meetings of the Japanese
Education Reform Council and its sub-committees.
f. Draft of Basic Law:
The Ministry of Education should prepare a draft of
a basic law of education in conformance with the concept outlined
above.

IV. THE SCHOOL SYSTEM
(Decisions made at the 16th General Meeting, 20 December 1946)
1. The Middle School (Grades 7-9, name tentative).
a. A three-year school on the lower secondary level, com-
prising grades 7, 8, and 9 should be established contiguous to the
six-year elementary school for the purpose of extending the basic
education of the people.
The school is tentatively referred to as the "Middle
School".
b. This school should be a full-time one, organized on a
coeducational basis, and attendance shall be compulsory.
c. It should be housed in its own separate building.
d. It should be staffed with full-time principals and
teachers.
e. Such a school should be established in every city, town,
or village.
f. To establish equality of opportunity in education, this
should be the only type of school contiguous to the elementary school
which comprises grades 1-6.
g. This school comprising grades 7-9 should be established
1 April 1947.
h. Appropriate economic measures should be taken to establish
this school.
2. The Three-Year Koto-Gakko (Grades 10-12, name tentative).
a. A three-year school on the upper secondary level, com-
prising grades 10, 11, and 12 should be established, with provision
made so that it may become either a 4-year or a 5-year school.
The school is tentatively referred to as the Koto Gakko.
b.
This type of school should offer either full-time or part-
time work.
c. It may be either coeducational or non-coeducational.
d. It may offer both general education courses and vocational
courses.
e. All graduates of the lower secondary school, grades 7-9,
under 18 years of age, should receive general education for at least
a prescribed minimum number of hours e ch year.
3. Institutions

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's 840,
January 29, 1947.
-8-
3. Institutions of Higher Learning. (Decisions made at the
17th General Meeting, 27 December 1946).
a. Schools contiguous to the three-year Koto Gakko should
be four-year institutions in principal, with provision that there also
may be three-year and five-year institutions on this level.
b. Post-graduate courses or institutes should be established
in universities to receive those who have graduated from the university
and wish to continue study.
4. Education of Teachers. (Decision made at the 17th General
Meeting, 27 December 1946).
a. Education for teachers should be provided by institutions
of higher learning which have only one faculty or which have several,
by establishment of an educational course of study in the institution.

V. PRIVATE SCHOOLS
(Decisions made at the 17th General Meeting, 27 December 1946).
In order to place private schools on a firm social and
financial basis it is desired: (1) That these schools be given
assistance in the sound development of school administration;
(2) That these schools maintain public and democratic character-
istics; (3) That these schools be made special juridical persons
separate from juridical persons as established by civil law.
In establishing the school juridical person law the
following points should be considered:
1. Private schools should be permitted to carry on profit-
yielding enterprises, the profits to be used in operating the schools,
insofar as the enterprises do not hinder the educational activities
of the schools.
2. Membership of the boards of directors should include
some professional educators.
3. Private schools should originate such democratic pro-
cedures as establishment of a board of councillors fram the school
staff having responsibility to answer inquiries of the school board
of directors and to submit its opinions to the school board.
4. Officials of private schools should be regarded by law
as public servants on the same basis as are officials of public
schools.
5. Private schools should be exempted from taxes and should
receive other financial assistance.
6. The right of government officials to dissolve private
school juridical persons and to dismiss private school officials
from their posts should be acknowledged. But such corporations or
persons should have the right both to file petition and to initiate
legal proceedings. In case of either action, the execution of the
order should be suspended.
7. A committee, including representatives selected from an
Association of Private Schools, should be formed which can answer
inquiries of the supervising governmental officials and submit opinions
to them.
8. Supervision by government officials should be conducted
along lines yet to be determined by the Sub-Committee on Educational
Administration.

=== Page 160 === (confidence 1.00)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's 840,
January 29, 1947.
-10-
VI. EDUCATIONAL ADMINISTRATION
(Decisions made at 17th General Meeting, 27 December 1946).
1. Fundamental Points in Reform.
Fundamental reform of educational administration should
be made, and special stress should be laid upon the following
points:
a. Rectification of hitherto customary bureaucratic
standardization and formalism.
b. Attachment of importance to the just opinion of
citizens in educational administration.
c. Confirmation of autonomy of education and decentrali-
zation of educational administration.
d. Establishment of close connection between various levels
of the formal educational system and social education.
e. Attachment of importance to studies and researches con-
cerning education.
f. Adjustment of educational finance.
2. Administrative Units.
In conformity with the above principles, the educational
administration should be independent of local civil administration
as far as possible, and it must be carried out by the autonomous
educational organizations of the people. For that purpose the
following suggestions are made:
a. In each village, town, city, and prefecture an educational
committee composed of members elected from among citizens should be
organized to make decisions concerning education. Each education
committee should elect an educational president (tentative title -
duties same as those of superintendent of schools in the United States)
as the person responsible for carrying out the recommendations of the
education committee.
as a rule, this education committee should have charge
of both formal school administration and social education within its
area of jurisdiction. The committee should also concern itself with
such matters as the establishment, abolition, and operation of schools;
contents of education; personnel administration; and educational
finance. As it has close relationship with the local civil adminis-
tration, especially concerning local finance, every consideration
should be given to carrying out its responsibilities in harmonious
relationship with the local civil administration.
b. In

=== Page 161 === (confidence 1.00)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's 840,
January 29, 1947.
-11-
b. In administration of higher educational institutions,
the prefectural areas are too small to function as independent units.
A more satisfactory unit could be established by the union of several
prefectures to form a district for purposes of higher educational
administration, which district could function through two organizations:
(1) a district educational committee, and (2) a district educational
research institute. These committees should rectify the disparity of
contents of education and of educational finance among various pre-
fectures. They should plan appropriate administration of educational
personnel.
Prefectural education committees within the district should
elect members of a district educational committee to be set up to repre-
sent that area.
The district educational research institute should carry out
investigation and research concerning education in conformity with local
situations, and should, on basis of its findings, advise educational
authorities of villages, towns, cities, and prefectures within the
district.
c. Each large metropolis should also establish a central edu-
cational committee to assume responsibility for study of important
problems, and to supply information to the Minister of Education.
d. The above plan for educational administration can be
diagrammed as follows:

3. Financial Support.
a. The educational conmittee of a village, town, city
or prefecture should be authorized to request authorities of its
self-governing body to add to its general estimated budget a
specified sum of money considered necessary for local education.
b. Although the cost of compulsory education can properly
be borne by local self-governing bodies, for the time being in view
of the present condition of local finances, the greater part of the
expenditure should be disbursed from the national treasury.
c. Funds for expenditure by district educational committees
should be disbursed from the national treasury as a means of equal-
izing the disparity of educational expenditure among prefectures.
d. Because local educational expenses will increase in the
future, certain kinds of taxes should be transferred to the local
bodies to increase their revenue.
e. The national as well as the local public education
organizations should endeavor to maintain basic sources of revenue
in order to meet the educational needs.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
The Terms of Reference of the Far Eestern Commisadon provide that
one of the functions of the Commission should be to "formulate the policies,
principles and standards in conformity with which the fulfillment by Japan
of its obligations under the Terms of Surrender may be accomplished."
It is further provided that when such decisions are made by the
Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government shall prepare direc-
tives in accordance with the policy decisions of the Commission and shall
transmit them to the Supreme Commander through the appropriate United
States Government agency."
At the fifty-first meeting of the Far Eastern Commission held at
2516 Massachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington, D. C., on 27 March 1947,
the enclosed policy decision relative to Revision of the Japanese Educa-
tional System was unanimously approved.
As Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I have been
instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of the Commission in
order that the appropriate directives may be prepared and transmitted to
the Supreme Commander in accordance with the Terms of Reference.
Before the enclosed policy decision was formally approved by the
Commission, the Australian Representative made a statement setting forth
CS/V
the views of his Government with regard to the decentralization of the
Japanese educational system, and the Indian Representative commented upon
paragraphs 2 and 7 of the enclosure. It was agreed that these statements
be recorded in the minutes and be trensmitted to the Supreme Commander for
his information. Excerpts from the draft minutes of the Commission's
fifty-first meeting, including these statements verbatim, are enclosed.
The Commission has accordingly instructed me to ask that the United States
Government in transmitting the appropriate directive based on the enclosed
policy decision to the Supreme Commander, also forward the Australian and
Indian statements for the Supreme Commander's information.
In adopting the enclosed policy decision the Commission agreed
that it should be released to the press after being received by the Supreme

ZNCLOSURE
Praft Daedler 
POLICY FOR THE REVISION OF THE JAPANESE EDUCATIONAL SYSTEL
Guiding Principles and Objectives.
1. Education should be looked upon as the pursuit of truth, as
preparation for life in a democratic nation, and as a training for the
social and political responsibilities which freedom entails. Emphasis
should be placed on the dignity and worth of the individual, on indopen-
dent thought and initiative, and on developing a spirit of inquiry.
The inter-dependent character of international life should be stressed.
The spirit of justice, fair play, and respect for the rights of others.
particularly minorities, and the necessity for friendship based upon
mutuel respect for people of all races and religions, should be empha-
sized. Special emphasis should also be placed on the teaching of the
sanctity of the pledged word in all human relations, whether between
individuals or nations. Measures should be taken as rapidly as possible
to achieve equality of educational opportunity for all regardless of sex
or social position. The revision of the Japanese educational system
should in large measure be undertaken by the Japanese themselves and steps
should be taken to carry out such revision in accordance with the princi-
ples and objectives set forth in this paper.
Training, Recruitment, and Conditions of Service of Teachers
2. Those teachers and other educational officials whose record shows
them to have been pronounced exponents of ultra-nationalistic, militaris-
tic, or totalitarian ideas, should be forbidden to teach or engage in
other employment connected with education.
3. Short refresher courses and vacation schools for teachers
should be opened, so far as possible, in order to train them in-democratic
ideas.
4. The development of modern techniques of teaching should be en-
couraged and opportunities should be provided for teachers to become ac-
quainted with these techniques. In this connection, regard should be had
to the value of affording teachers opportunities of transferring from one
institution to another.
5. As a corrective to the regimented and limiting nature of normal
school training in the past, and to provide teachers with aims and tech-
niques in harmony with the objectives of the ocqupation, special emphasis
should be placed upon the reorganization of normal schools and the estab-
lishment of teachers training institutes staffed with the most competent
instructors available for inculcating democratic principles. Endeavors
should be made to increase the proportion of teachers who have had a
university training.
-1-
FEC-092/1

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
6. The teaching profession should be recognised as of vital
its economic status should be improved to a degree commensurate with this
importance. Consideration should be given to the establishment of malary
scales affording all teachers a reasonable stendard of living according to
their abilities, qualifications, and responsibilities without the mecossity
of supplementing their income from outside sources. A basic living wage
should be guaranteed for all teachers, with increases according to their
qualifications.
Textbooks. Curricula and Teaching Methods.
7. Teaching of ultra-nationalism, state Shintoism, veneration of the
Emperor, exaltation of the state over the individual, and race superiority,
should be eliminated from the educational system.
8. Textbooks and other reading material thet contain such ideas as
those outlined above should be withdrawn from use in schools. New text-
books should be issued which give an understanding of progressive ideas.
Foreign books should be made available, especially in central libraries,
and for teachers. These objectives should be given due weight when alloca-
tions of paper supplies and imports of foreign publications are made.
9. Courses in social sciences, civics, constitutional law and govern-
ment, current events, world affairs and international cooperation should be
made an integral part of the educational system at appropriate levels of
teaching.
10. Teaching of military subjects should be totally forbidden in all
educational institutions. The wearing by students of military-style uniforms
should be forbidden. Classical sports, such as KENDO, which encourage the
martial spirit, should be totelly abandoned. Physical training should no
longer be associated with the SEISHIN KYOIKU. Greater emphasis should be
placed on games and other recreational activities than on pure calisthenics
and drill. If former servicemen are employed as drill instructors, or in
connecticn with physical training or sport, they should be carefully
screened.
1l. Imperial rescripts should not be used as a basis of instruction,
study, or ceremonies in schools.
12.
. Independent thinking on the part of teachers and students should
be encouraged.
13. Uniform minimum etandards should be prescribed for the different
levels of instruction in all the schools of Japan, whether public or private.

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
Adult Bducation.
14. Adult education should be promoted rapidly by the use of all
suitable facilities such as evening classes, university extension courses.
the radio, the cinema, and libraries.
Vocational Education.
15. Japanese youth should be provided with opportunities for varied
vocetional training and guidance and appropriate organizations for this
purpose.
Educational Administration and Finance.
16. The Japanese Government should seek advice from representatives
of all walks of life either through a non-official advisory council or
otherwise.
17. The Japanese Government should exercise such control over the
education system as will ensure the achievement of the objectives of the
occupation, particularly the reforms called for by this policy decision.
Subject to the foregoing, and to maintenance of standards prescribed by
the Government, the responsibilty for the local administration of educa-
tional establishments should in due time be decentralized. Japanese
parents and citizens should be encouraged to feel a sense of individual
responsibility for the achievements of the objectives set out in paragraph
1. Where practicable they should be associated with the control, develop-
ment, and work of the schools and other educational institutions.
18. The plans enumerated in this peper should be closely correlated
with the reforms in the social, economic and political life of the nation.
In the implementation of the educational policies outlined above, funds
should be allocated for all essential educational reform commensurate with
the needs and resources of the nation.
19. In order that educational standards in poor districts should not
be lowered by tne inability of some local bodies to provide sufficient
finance from local revenue, finance for education should come for the most
part from the national government, which should be responsible for the
maintenance of an adequate level of education throughout Japan. Local and
private bodies should be encouraged to supplement these funds provided by
the national government.
General.
20. Free and compulsory education should be provided for all Japanese
children for a minimum period of six yeare and should be extended to higher
age groups as rapidly as poesible.
21. More opportunities should be provided for higher education.

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
22. Equal opportunity for both sexes should be provided at all
levels of education -- primary, secondary, and tertiary.
23. Encouragement should be given to the formation and reorientation
of educational associations, parent-teacher associations; and to assist in
making the Japanese people aware of the significant changes in the diree-
tion of education in a democratic Japan, such groups should be encouraged
to consider practical problems of education.
24. Discrimination against the graduates of private schools in civil
service appointments should be eliminated, provided the schools in question
conform to educational standards laid down for the public educational system.
25. Educational institutions of foreign foundation in Japan have
played a useful part in the past in widening and deepening the scope of
Japanese education, and should be given equal rights to those of Japanese
institutions in future.

GERPTS FROM THE MINUTES OF THE FIFTY-PIRST MEETING OF THE FAR E
27 MARCH 1947
ITEM 5 - POLICY FOR THE REVISION OF THE JAPANESE EDUCATIONAL BYSTEM
(PEC-092/1)
Statement by the Indian Representative:
Mr. Chairman, I am grateful to you for having allowed me this
opportunity to discuss things in conference with you and your educational
adviser on the subject. I would not like to take up the time of the
Commission on this paper. I find that it is a most excellent paper, drafted
by those who know the subject. They have taken all relevant questions into
consideration and have dealt with them as well as they could in the ciroum-
stances. When I say in the circumstances I mean that Japan today represents,
along with some of the others whose fate is being dealt with elsewhere, a
country and a people who unfortunately were a little taoexcessive in their
ambition. They were carrying out their ambition in a form which had become
a menace to the world, which menace fortunately has now been conquered.
And now we must be very concerned that that menace is not revived in any
shape or form anywhere--not merely in Japan but elsewhere too. If the
world is to preserve peace, and if humanity is to grow along lines of
peaceful prosperity, then these tendencies have got to be restrained wherever
they may be found. That is the object behind this paper and I am entirely
in agreement with it. But there are one or two little points which have
occurred to me after a superficial reading of this paper in the short time
I have had at my disposal, and which I have mentioned to the Chairman. With
the help of his advice my doubts have been more or less satisfied for the
time being. But it is only relevant that I should mention these points to
you now so that you may also bear in mind how my mind is moving.
Now, if you look at page 1, paragraph 2, the opening sentence runs
as follows: "Those teachers and other educational officials whose record
shows them to have been pronounced exponents of ultra-nationalistic, mili-
taristic, or totalitarian ideas, should be forbidden to teach or engage in
other employment...." Please mark the word "employment" there, "...or en-
gage in other employment connected with education." Now, supposing for
instance, little children are being taught in schools and some of the se men
are available for supplying, shall we say, milk to the children, which is
possibly a. connected activity. After all we must see that the children are
brought up properly, and we supply them with milk. You know what the man-
power shortage is like all over the world. Supposing these people can be
employed in that particular activity, which will still be connected with
education but not education itself. I want to know whether this will bar
them. If so, I think we are drawing rather heavily upon our resources of
manpower. That is one doubt. I don't know whether that doubt has been
completely resolved, but I do not wish that little point to become a hin-
drance in the passage of this paper.

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.97)
-2
Paragraph 7, relating to textbooks, curricula, and teaching methods
says: "Teaching of ultra-nationalism, State Shintoism....should be eliminated
from the educational system." Now I fear that the expression "State Shintoism
infringes a principle, a fundamental principle, not that I for one second
desire Shintoism to be revived anywhere in that form, because, after all,
militarist Shintoism has certainly proved a very dreadful thing as far as
Japan is concerned. I have not the slightest doubt about it and nobody would
like it to be revived in that form. But it is a question of a people -
I am going to the fundamentals--claiming a teaching to be religious. Are we
today sanctioning that principle? Are we telling anyone todey that religion
in any shape or form or in any particular form shall not become State religion
anywhere? What about Christianity? There are certain countries where Chris-
tianity is a State religion. The United Kingdom has a form of Christianity
as a State religion. Later on you may find it difficult, after having laid
down a law or principle like this, you may find it difficult to defend it.
I am only warning you. That is about all. It is quite possible that some-
body may come up afterwards and say no religion shall ever be taught anywhere
as a State religion. Personally I may be quite prepared to subscribe to it
because religion and State should never be confused. It is a wrong thing
to do. But it is a point for you to consider. It is easy for us to lay
down certain rules and fundamentals in a hurry and then find later on that
they become halters around our necks. Thi- is a doubt which I have expressed.
中
幸
Statement by the Australian Representative:
The Far Eastern Commission has not laid down in any detail in
paragraphs 16, 17, 18, and 19 of FEC-092/1 how the Japanese educational sys-
tem is to be decentralized. It has been felt that the Commission should
do no more than lay down general policy, in order that the Supreme Commander
will have the greatest possible degree of discretion in determining what is
most suitable in the light of current circumstances. The Australian Govern-
ment fully agrees with this, and hes complete confidence in General Mac-
Arthur. However, the Austral ian Government would like to state formally its
more detailed views, and to ask that these be sent to General MacArthur for
his guidence in the degree and timing of decentralization, the Supreme Com-
mander of course retaining full discretion as to the extent to which he
follows these lines.
It is believed that comprehensive educational reform is likely to
be most effectively handled by a centralized Government department. There-
fore decentralization of administration is not recommended for general policy
planning, finance, appointment of teachers, conditions for teacherg, provision
of buildings, planning of educational standards, and the over-all conduct
and planning of research programmes. However, the carrying out of details
of administration should be decentralized. This decentralization might be
accomplished most effectively by placing local administration of education
under district superintendents of education, appointed by the

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
Japanese Government, who should be educationalists of standing. It is
believed that the schools and teachers should not be under the montrel
the prefectural administrations. In determining curricula, a central
authority should make general suggestions for all subjects and should
specify requirements of standards; however, within those limits, decents
sation should occur, and is recommended particularly for social studies,
biological sciences and vocational subjects in which local conditions
should play an important role.
It is not considered that the Japanese people, particularly in
rural and remote areas, are yet fitted to undertake the responsibility of
local supervision of education through educational committees or boards
elected under local franchise. Nor is it considered that the teaching
body as a whole is fitted to accept immediately the freedoms in determining
curricula and teaching methods implied in a decentralized system. The pro-
cess of decentralization should take place gradually over a period of some
years and should keep pace with the progress of development of democratic
thought among teachers and parents.
Any sudden change-over from central control to complete decen-
tralization might result in chaos, in giving too great control in remote
districts to reactionary and ignorant elements of the population, and in
making difficult the Supreme Commander's supervision and inspection of
educational reform.

Statement by the Indian Representative:
Mr. Chairman, I am grateful to you for having allowed
me this opportunity to disouss things in conference with
you and your educational adviser on the subjeet. I would
mot like to take up the time of the Commission on this
paper. I find that it is a most exesllent paper, drafted
by those who know the subjeot. They have taken all rele-
vant questions into consideration and have dealt with them
as well as they could in the ciroumstances. When I say in
the ciroumstances I mean that Japan today represents,
along with some of the others whose fate is being dealt
with elsewhere, a country and a people who unfortunately
were a little too excessive in their ambition. They were
carrying out their ambition in a form whieh had besome a
menace to the world, which menace fortunately has now been.
conquered. And now we must be very coneerned that that
menace is not revived in any shaps or form anywhere--mot
merely in Japan but elsewhere too. If the world is to
preserve peace, and if humanity is to grow along lines of
peaceful prosperity, then these tendenoies have got to be
restrained wherever they may be found. That is the objeet
behind this paper and I am entirely in agreement with it.
But there are one or two little points which have eceurred
to me after a superficial reading of this paper in the
short time I have had at my disposal, and which I have men-
tioned to the Chairman. with the help of his adviee my
doubts have been more or less satisfied for the time being.
But it is only relevant that I should mention these points
to you now so that you may also bear in mind now ay mind
1s moving.

educational officials whose record shows them to have been

Statement by the Australian Representative:
Japanese educational systen is to be deseatralised. It has
will have the greatest possible degree of dissretiem in
determining what is most suitable in the light of surremt
ciroumstances. The Australian Government fully agrees wish
this, and has complete confidence in General Maearthur.
However, the Australian Government would like to stete fermal-
ly its more detailed views, and to ask that these be semt
to General MaoArthur for his guidance in the degree asd
timing of decentralization, the Supreme Commander of courss
retaining full disoretion as to the extent to whieh he
follows these lines.
It is believed that comprehensive educational refomm
is likely to be most effectively handled by a centralised
Government department. Therefore decentralization of ad-
finance, appointment of teachers, conditions for teashers,
provision of buildings, planning of educational standards,
and the over-all conduct and planning of researeh pregrammes.
However, the oarrying cut of details of administratiem shoul
be decentralized. This decentralization might be acsomplisked
most effectively by placing local administration of edusation
under distriot superintendents of education, appeinted by the
Japanese Government, who should be educationalists of stand-
ing. It is believed that the schools and teachers should
not be under the control of the prefeotural administratioas.
In determining curricula, a central authority should make
gemeral suggestions for all subjects and should speeify re-
quirements of standards; however, within those limits, de-
centralization should ocour, and is recommended particularly
for sooial studies, biological soienees and vocational sub-
jeots in which local conditions mhould play an importaat rele.
It is not considered that the Japanese people, mrtioular-
ly in rural and remote areas, are yet fitted to undertake
the responsibility of local supervision of education through
educational committees or boards elected under local framehise.
Nor is it considered that the teaching body as a whele is
fitted to accept immediately the freedoms in determining
ourricula and teaching methods implied in a deceatralised
system. The process of decentralizetion should take place
gradually over a period of some years and should keep pace
with the progress of development of democratie thought among
teaohers and parents.
Any

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.97)
Any sudden change-over from central control to eem-
plete decentralization might result in chaos, in siving
toe great control in remote distriets to reactionary and
igmorant elements of the population, and in naking dirri-
oult the Supreme Conmander's supervision and iaspeetien
of educational reform.

Washington 25, D. C.
Dear Mr. Holland:
Thank you for you letter of July 9 with the enclosed
communication from Lady Butterfield and the essay on
Atomic Energy and World Peace. As you know, the effects
of Atomic Energy are far too recent and impressive in
the minds of the Jepanese people for us to need to give
any emphasis to this subject. As a matter of fact, we are
trying to re-build Japan in terms of the Constitution
Promulgated in May, which outlaws war for the Japanese
people for all times.
We are making some progress in the development of
education in Japan, tut are confronted with serious
shortages of teechers, buildings, books and instructional
materials which make the work exceedingly difficult.
With all good personel wishes, I am
Very cordially yours,

GENFRAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLTED POWERS
Civil Information and Education Section
Media Analysis Division
No:94
PUBLICATIONS ANALYSIS, 12 March 1947
Teachers and Teaching Methods
There seems to be no more enthusiastic group of Japanese
writers than those who appear in educational megazines or dis-
cuss educational subjects in other publications. Most of these
writers are themselves teachers or educt tional officials. Articles
on the general subject of education are noteworthy, for they show
considerable comprehension of the resnonsibility of the schools in
creating a democratic nation. In the more detailed writing, teach-
ers seem inclined to discuss their particular fields -- especially
their own theories, methods and projects -- in a way which indicates
considerable individual thinking and enthusiasm. Because of the
abundance of material dealing with the field of education, it has
seemed advisable to limit this report to the subject of teaching
and teaching methods and to matters which are closely allied.
A Chiba primary school director, Kanzo Komura, declares in
the educationel journal KOKUMIN KYOIKU (Kogakunen), that "Educa-
tion Must Roconstruct Japan." The Japanese must "set a far-
sighted policy in the present chaotic oircumstances. This must
be based on experience and actual pructice as well as on science
and philosophy. * * * Our education, having been set free from
its wartime status, must be reappraised from the viewpoint of world

The Japanese educational world "at present needs most the
spirit of independence and freedom from narrow-mindedness," Takeshi
Koide, a primary school principal, states in KOKUMIN KYOIku(Tei-
gakunen). The lack of a spirit of independence is manifested in
many ways at the present time, and is the result of the years of
oppression; but henceforth the Japanese must cease casting recrimi-
nations and must be anxious to join with other nations in contri-
buting to world civilization. To illustrate thc new outlook
which he feels should be purveyed through the schools, Koide as-
serts that the Pacific Ocean should serve to join Japan with the
rest of the world, rather than to keep it secluded.
Advice to the younger generation is offered by Professor Kenko
Kimura of Tokyo Imperial University,in SEkAI. Establishment of a
democratic system is not al that is required, he writes. "If
people know only how to submit, and do not know how to think and
act on their own volition, a democratic system will turn out to
be worse than useless." What is most important, declares Kimura
and what must be achieved through education, is "the spirit of
independence-the ability to act on one's own initiative without
compulsion from outside and with the spirit of self-responsibility."

Teaching Methods and Media
In ASAHI HYORON Teiyu Amano, director of tho Tirst Higher
School, surveys the educational ficld, and points out certain
shortcomings of the old syst ns: (1) education had no independence
and was under the continued sway of the politicians and the
militarists; (2) the formality of oducation wes considered more
important than tho subject matter and the effects of the teaching;
(3) it was felt that the object of education was simply to pre-
pare one for worldly success; (4) it was commonly believed that
the studicus attitude should be dropped upon graduation from school
or college, rather than that cducation should furnish the found-
ation upon which one could continue to study and build further
knowledge; (5) it was difficult for students to tronsfer from
one school to another, and the differences between government and
private schools were such that boys were discouragcd from attend-
ing private institutions. Amano urges that education be given
its independence - with the management of educational metters left
entirely in the hands of educators, that prefectural schools be
freed from central control, and that the authority of the direct-
ors and principals of cach school or college be extended He
would standardize all education through the middle school years
(he advocates a six year primary school course and four years of
middle school) with a general proparatory course from which 
students could embark casily upon a more specialized higher edu-
cation.

Textbooks
The Jananese have rolied too heavily on textbooks, Muneomi.
Kaigo of Tokyo Inperial University ncintains in NIPPON KYOIKU.
"Once the character of textbooks was established, a tradition deve-
loned, and revision has been lifficult," he declares. "The charact-
er of textbooks has a 'close relationship with the prevailing edu-
cational methods." In Jananese education, the method of teaching
always has considered the convenience of the teachers, not the
students. What the teachers nresented was strictly limited to what
was found in the book, and this the students learned by heart,
"neglecting not one word from start to finish." Now the approach
must be radically changed, Kaigo writes, and Janan must have "not
books for teaching, but books for learning." Workbooks and books
for self-study are pronosed by the writer, who exnlains that such
books would have passages of descrintion, followed by questions,
and that answering these questions should occupy much of the
student's study cffort.
The writer then divides the educational process into three
stages. In the first the student acquires the neans for master-
ing the contents of the second stage; in the second he learns such
subjects as civics, geograrhy, history, etc.; and in the third he
anplies his knowledge to living materials. In the first stage text-
books are necessary and the teacher's work should be closely related
to these broks. In the second stage, the student should use text-
books designed for self-study -- "textbooks for learning, not teach-
ing"; and in the third, "liberal study through living subjects" in-
volves the use of materials and as many reference books as are avail
able. Kaigo also holds that teachers must be provided freedom to
bring their own exneriences and ideas into play and to con ile their
own material.

Placing all education uron a scientific basis is advocated by

ing for standards on the basis of known facts. Science is not
sim ly knowledge of natural nhenomena, but the method of induo-
tion of general laws from individual facts. "It is to abstract,
to induce, to organize, and then to analyze com lex henomena,
to penetrate external factors, to search for causes, and to extend
the annlications." As such, it should be studied and epnlied
through all levels and branches of society. In conclusion,Kuroda

new work. * * * A trained worker engaged in modern industry should

knowledge of the principles involved." Present-day Janan has need

the program attractive to children and the relationship between
proper nourishment and rhysical exertion. He thinks that a school
lunch program should be instituted as quickly as possible.
The Director of the Physical Training Promotion Section of
the Ministry of Elucation, Yoshihiko Kurimoto, writes on the manage-
ment of athletic meets in KOKUMIN KYOIKU: (Teigagunen).These events.
he declares, can give the students a sense of rleasure and satis-
faction which is especially necessary in the present dark days. He
says that management shoula be taken from the hands of the teachers
and given to the students, who will form good habits by assuming
jobs, taking resuonsibi ity for them, and carrying them out by in-
dividual initiative. This can bo an important part of democratic
education, Kurimoto holds, and ho follows with concrete suggestions
for successful management of group meets.
Conclusion
It is easy to perceive the enthusiasm, sometimes anproaching
fervor, which animates most of these educational writers. It might
scem especially encouraging to note that most of them are persons
actually in direct contact with the youth of Janan -- the teachers
themselves, who know tho rroblems firsthand and arise to meet the
challenge. It is evident that progressive thought is stirring in
Jananese educational circles.

Dear Mr. Schuler:
In the Thursday, 13 November 1947, issue of the New York Times there
is a brief notice datelined Tokyo, November 12, special to the New York
Times, entitled "New xchange Rate Is Urged for Japan".
Buried in this news items is a brief statement to the effect that
William J. Sebald, the American chairman of the Allied Council in Japan,
had "challenged the occupation plans for decentralizing the school ad-
ministration, asserting that finance and general policy, including the
appointment, promotion and dismissal of teachers and textbook prepara-
tion should remain under the central government". To say that I am
astonished is to put it mildly, as it was my impression that (1) this
policy of decentralization was the most fundamental recommendation (with
the possible exception of language revision) of the United States Educa-
tion Mission to Japan; (2) that this policy was approved by the United
States vepartment of State; (3) that the Far bastern Commission in Wash-
ington had approved this policy in its 27 March 1947 "Policy for the Re-
vision of the Japanese Educational System"; and (4) that it was made a
part of the 25 and 27 March 1947 Japanese Laws Implementing the New Con-
stitution, popularly known as the "Educational Reform Bills".
As this positin  Mr. Sebald, if it reflects official policy and if
it is accurately reported, marks the most fundamental break in the entire
Occupation policy of the United States, it would appear to be of funda-
CS
mental importance. Is it possible for you to indicate (1) whether or
not it is an accurate reporting of what he said; (2) whether or not there
V
has been this fundamental shift in Occupation policy; and (3) if the
United States has not shifted its position, why our official representa-
DEC19
tive used a Council meeting as a sounding board?
FILHD
Very truly yours,
0
taber
27
ROBERT KING HALL
