suBject: Your request for NA's comments on truth of attached New York
Times Article.
In reply to your request, I understand that Mr. Green
894
telephoned you several days ago to say that NA had fairly good
evidence that Mr. Sebald did not make the remarks attributed
to him by the New York Times but that a full reply could not be made
to your inquiry until the official minutes of the November 12 ACJ
meeting are received.
We have now received these minutes and from them it is clear
that Mr. Sebald made no statement that could be construed as veri-
fying the New York Times charge. However, at the close of the
-1747
meeting the British member, Mr. Shaw, made the following statement:
"In your report you describe the reorganization which has
been carried out under the provislons of the Constitution, the
Fundamental Law of Education, and the School Education Law. You
mentioned the extension of responsibilities from the national
to the prefectural levels, and the utilization of new professional
groups and local committees. This trend of decentralization of
educational activity is a healthy one, especially in the spheres
you particularly mentioned, namely, adult education and youth
organizations.
"Might I suggest, however, that the broad foundations of
educational reform must be laid by a centralized authority.
Important 1ssues such as the allocation of finance and the es-
tablishment of a regularized system for the appointment, promo-
tion and dismissal of teachers--such matters should remain among
the functions of a central-government department.
"It is 1mportant to tap local opinion and interest upon which
democratic ideas must ultimately be based, but it 1s rather danger-
ous to assume as yet that local provincial bodies would be as keen
for reform as the leading educators in the big cities.
Fw894.
"Policy planning and the supervision of textbooks should
therefore remain in the hands of a central education department
S
for some time yet."
c
It is therefore apparent that the New York Times erroneously
2
ascribed to Mr. Sebald what, in faety was stated by the British
member.

suBject: Attached file relating to a N.Y. Timesarticle, reporting

1e erroneous:

remarks made by Mr. Sebald at the aforementioned meeting.
This is an advance copy and apparently the only one in
Washington, having been brought back from Tokyo by Niles Bond.

N.Y. Times allegation that Mr. Sebald had "challenged the
Occupation plans for decentralizing the school administration".
In fact Mr. Sebald advocated quite the opposite.

representatives on the ACJ are always spoiling forshowdown
with Mr. Sebald on educational reforms." If Mr. Sebald had
made the remarke ascribed to him by the N.Y. Times, subsequent
FBIB Bulletins would have undoubtedly been replete with
indignant outpourings from the Soviet and Chinese press
and radio. This has not been the case.
Would you prefer that a memo be drafted in reply to Schuler's
inquiry or, because of the tentative nature of our reply, that
the foregoing info be 'phoned to him ?

THE SCHOOL OF THE SOVIET LAND
(Excerpts from 1: col. leading article)
In capitalist countries the school is the instrument of
bourgeois class domination.
For instance, in such "advaneed" capitalist countries as
England and imerica, under the veil of false denocratic phrases we see the
same systen of education as in Tsarist Russia completely subordinatéd to the
interests and policies of the ruling classes. School education is adapted
to prove the "pemanency" of the capitalist order. Some text-books, expeoially
geographical and historical ones, contain anti-Soviet propa cnde and malioious
slander against the Soviet people. This is how the principle of the
"independence" of the school frcn politics looks in reality! Under the
cover of this principle American "educators" moke use not only of their own
schools but those of other countries in the interests of the imperialist
poliey of their country. Thus, under th influence of such "educators"
education in China is  sed on American models and pupils are inspired with
submission to everything American. In Japan, the American occupational
authorities encourege the education of the youth in the old military spirit
and democretically inclined teachers are subject to persecution and oppression.
It would be possible to cite many examples of this kind.
The schools of the Soviet Union train active builders of
socialisn. For the first time ithhigtpry the problem of educeting not masters
and servants but a race of free people? has been raised and is being success
fully solved;
"Teachers' Gazette", No.44. 1947
WHAT INTERFERES WITH THE DEMOCRATISATION OF EDUCATION
IN JAPAN
By S. Maev (Summary of 1gcol. article)
Two years have passed since the capitulation of Japan. a
sufficient space of time in which to form the basis for the demoeratio re-
construction of this country. Even American conmentators, who can hardly be
accused of a special desire to criticise MacArthur's policy in Jepan, are
forced to adnit that the United States has not solved the obligations under-
taken befcre the United Nations.
The main lines for the denocratisation of the Japanese
educational system were laid down by the resolution of the Far Eastern
Canmission in March 1947.
It is apparent that the Japanese Government is mainly con-
cerned with sabotaging the denocratisation of Japanese education as well as
the demilitarisation of the country. the liguidation of large capitalist
concerns, the landed aristocracy, etc. Facts prove that the American
occupational authorities are not only failing to prevent the activity of
Japanese reaction, but are even encouraging it.
It is frequently al'eged that military education has been
abolished; mevertheless, with the support and encouragement of Mac-Arthur's
staff en organisation of bo scouts has recently been organised.
Of late the increased influence of the Catholie Church in
Japan is to be noted. Catholic schools and colleges with funds fram abroad
are in a position to affer better conditions than Japanese schools and are
thus attracting a considerable number of pupils. It is needless to say that
this educational aotivity is not so much of a religious as of. a political nature.
The missionarics spare no effort to divert Japanese youth
from the struggle for tho domocratic reorganization of the cóuntry. The
Amorican church is also cndeavouring to strengthen its position. Early in
1947 the Council of tho United Church of America and Canada docided to
grant 10 million dollars for the building of an International Christian
University in Japan.

"Teachers' Gazette" Nos.41-44, 1947.
8. (Cont'a.)
With the support of the American occupation authorities
the Japanese Governnent is doing its best to undernine the denddratio
movement ariong Japanese teachers.
The struggle of the teachers to i prove their material
conditions is carried on through their trade union organisations - the
All-Japanese Congress of Teachers' Unions and the Teachers' Federation.
The l1-Japanese Congress in March 1947 numbered over 320,000 menbers and
is one of the biggest unions in the country. Here the cormunist
influence is considerable. The Teachers' Federation is a trade union
organisation which strives not to "ix" trade union activities with the
political struggle. The main role in the struggle. of the Japanese
teachers belongs cf course to the All-Japanese Congress of Teachers'
Unions.
One of the cormonest methods used by reactionaries to rid
thenselves of undesirable elenents is to accuse then of syupathy with
the 'cornunists. The Minister of Education in the Yoshida cabinet openly
declared in March this year that the Governnent objects to the
participation in plitical activity of teachers. who are sympathetic with
the coinunist party, and that the Government objects to their ideological
influence over pupils. At the sane time he hyppcritically declared that
freedon of thought is granted to cverybody in Japan.
The struggle for the denocratisation of education in
Japan is one of tho most important links in the struggle for the
denocratisation of Japan and the maintenance of. peace and security in
the Far East. The merican occupation authorities have taken the
path of supporting Japanese reaction in its endeavour to preserve the
conservative basis of Japanese education. This once more proves the
general character of American policy which is directed towards turning
Japan into a jumping-off ground for Anerican expansion in the Pacific,
not only for strategic and economic, but also for ideological purposes.

The task of educating the intelligentsia in the. spirit of

great in the realisation of the grandiose plans for the reconstruction of
national econony. Vigorous collective scientific efforts are necessary
for the successful sclution of complicated and complex scientific problens.
Special care should be taken to safeguard .Soviet soientifin priority.
Any sign of servility towards bourgeois science which serves its capitalist
masters should be corbatted.

"Publio Education", No.9, 194.7.
A VISIT TO TOKIO UNIVERSITY
By S. Maev (Exoerpts from 8-page article)
The writer describes a visit he paid to the Tokio Imperial
University. He and his party entered through the main entry; ignoring
the sign "Off limits", with the aid of which the merican authorities
would like to hide Japan and their own activities in Japan from the
rest of the world.
The writer remarks particularly on the gloomy, dilapidated
and deserted air of the whole building.
The leoture-halls are' dark
and dust covers the benches.
In thc litcrary faculty, a. separate room is set aside for
each different language - English, Frenoh and German. Here thero aro
complete editions of the Western classios, many dictionaries, various
Journals and papers. Among the German books and journals are not a
few of a fascist nature, which are in free oirculation. On the shelves
with the French books are placed all the Slav works - literature in the
Russian, Polish, Serb and Bulgarian languages -a few dozen books in all.
They inoluded Polcvoi's "History of Russian Literature", the collooted
works of Lermontov, somc editions of the works of Pushkin and Nekrasov,
a book of Balmont's verses, and a pre-revolutionary textbook of Russian
grammar. The books and shelves were covered with dust: there was no
indioation that thc books were used. Indeed, in none of these rooms
was there a student to be scen.
The same neglccted air prevailed in the other faoultics;
there was a lack of the most essential cquipment in the soientifio
laboratories.
On visiting the library, the writer was surprised to find
comparatively few Russian books - in fact, only the works of Chekhov
in the Russian language. On enquiry, it was discovered that all the
other Russian books before the war had been removed. and taken to an
underground cellar, since the Japanese government was afraid of the
spread of the "Bolshevik infection."
The Russian books were still lying in heaps on the cellar
floor, whereas Hitler's "Mein Kampf" is to be bought at Tokio's
bookstalls.
Suoh is the "democratisation" which is being carried on
in Japan.
A talk with the Rector of the University threw some light
on the hard conditions of living of the students. Moreover, on the
completion of their coursc of study, there is no guarantee that they
will find enployment. The Rector.corplained of the spirit of unrest
prevailing among the students, owing to which "special control" was
necessary.
He said that many students were unable to "understand
the leotures", owins: to their backwardness caused by the lack of
study in wartime. In fact, however, the writer suggests that the
reason is rather that for many years the youth has been taught in a
militaristic spirit, has been encouraged not to think independently.
As a result the students are not informed as to the most clementary
subjects of the culture of the West, international relations, eto.
At the University, as in other parts of the country,
a struggle is going on between the new and progressive and the feudal,
conservative past. The best students wish to live in a new way,
and that is why "special control" over them is required.
n /bmo

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
"Public Education", No.9, 1947
1. (Cont'a)
The University has a Communist organization and Komsomol
organization. In Novenber 1946 there were 45 Communists and 40 Komsomol
members in the University - the advance guard of the best seotion of
students.
The Communists publish their paper "Truth", while the
Komsomol have a wall newspaper.
There exists a very flcurishing cirole for the study of
the Sovict Union. Many members of this study the Russian language and
read Russian newspapers and journals, wishing to know the truth about
the Soviet Union, which has so long been hidden from them.
A student of the law faculty said that among other
organisations there is a oircle for the study and promoting of friendship
with the USi. But it exists only nominally for since its foundation,
several months ngo, it has shown no signs of life.
The conditions of professors and teachers of the University
must be mentioned. According to "Stars and Stripes" the average yearly
inoome of city professors in Japan is 5931 yen, that is, 494 yen monthly.
How can a man with a family live when he receives a monthly salary
sufficient only to buy a dozen handkerchiefs ?
In analysing the past and searching for new ways of
life, the better part of the Japanese soholars are turning their eyes
to the Soviet Union, wherc science serves the people and the soientist
is surrounded with attention and solicitude.
Interest in the USSR among Japanese scientists is daily
growing. Despite the obstaoles created by Macirthur, Japanese scientists
are acquainting themselves with the achicvements of Soviet science and
are astounded by the successes aohieved by the Soviet Union.
"Public Education", No.9, 1947.

Primary staff rosponsibility, intra-staff coordination and liaison
with Japancse Government agencies on policios dealing with Japanese
education, educational organization, scientific research and technology
are assigned as follows:
a. To the Economic and Scientific Section for the coordination
of matters dealirg with research and technclogy affecting more than
one section, and or those matters connected with science and research
in the natural'sciences not specifically assigned to other staff seotions.

nation of matters dealing with education and for all matters (research
includod) dealing with the social and cultural sciences.
c. To the Hatural Resources Section, Public Health and Welfare
Section, Civil Comnunications Section, Engineer Section and Civil Trans-
portation Section for matters (education and research) dealing with
those sciences specifically relating to their respective fields.
